### 13.61 Configurable Linux site-to-site TUN MTU (2026-10-05)

`tunGateway.mtu` is a Chimera-only extension, defaults to 1500 bytes, and accepts 1280–9000. The minimum preserves IPv6 link MTU requirements; the cap bounds packet buffers and matches common jumbo-frame deployments. The validated plan applies one value to the Linux TUN device, the receive buffer, `watfaq-netstack`'s smoltcp device capability, and IPv4/IPv6 UDP output fragmentation. Vendor `NetStack::new()` retains its 1500-byte behavior; `new_with_mtu` validates and propagates explicit values.

The server TUN memory integration exercises out-of-order IPv4 and IPv6 4 KiB fragments at 1280 bytes, including reassembly and the existing UDP/Mux boundary checks. Vendored tests verify 1280-byte output fragments in both IP families and expose the MTU through smoltcp capabilities. `TUN_GATEWAY_MTU=1280 bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_managed_routes_netns.sh` verifies that Linux creates the interface at the configured MTU and that managed IPv4/IPv6 TCP/UDP routes still work. Verification passed: `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --no-default-features --features tun-gateway,vless-reverse --lib tun_gateway::tests --locked` (24 tests), `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` (20 unit + 34 integration tests), vendor and workspace all-target/all-feature Clippy with warnings denied, and the namespace smoke above. This does not make `tunGateway` an Xray TUN schema or establish Xray TUN interoperability; site-gateway support remains Partial.

The full Reverse site path was subsequently rerun with `TUN_GATEWAY_MTU=1280 bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh` and passed. The real TUN interface and Office routes used MTU 1280; the scenario covered dual-stack LAN TCP/UDP, 4 KiB UDP, IPv4/IPv6 fragment admission pressure, three 62-second UDP idle-expiry recoveries, UDP iperf3 (375,600 bytes sent and received, 0% loss), Hub recovery, three Edge-only restarts, and TUN teardown. A 4 MiB TCP echo was byte-compared at Office and Edge and completed after client write-half-close; both sides reported 4,194,328 bytes and SHA-256 `4b7d55b3179211492095a48e98d3e3175c48a0f4ba9e27b934cd15a10f7d3f99`. The smoke now admits eight concurrent TCP flows, rejects the ninth before Edge, and verifies admission recovers. TCP iperf3 is not used as a pass criterion: previous runs sometimes received a control-result EOF after the Edge fixture had processed payload, so this smoke verifies TCP integrity and half-close but does not claim a TCP throughput result. This remains isolated Linux namespace evidence, not production LAN/load evidence or Xray TUN compatibility; support remains Partial.

The declared upper MTU bound was separately exercised with `TUN_GATEWAY_MTU=9000 bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_managed_routes_netns.sh`. The real TUN interface and managed IPv4/IPv6 routes reported 9000, forwarded TCP/UDP for Office sources, preserved Server-local LAN routing, and cleaned up after shutdown and startup rollback. This verifies managed-route operation at the upper bound; the full Reverse site topology has not been rerun at MTU 9000.

### 13.62 Reverse Bridge stale-session isolation and TCP iperf3 result recovery (2026-10-05)

The full TCP iperf3 probe intermittently transferred its payload but lost the final control result. The Bridge Mux reader treated a failed channel send to a logical session whose receiver had just exited as a physical-reader error, cancelling every session on that VLESS Reverse link. `KEEP` delivery now responds with a standard per-session `END` when the session disappears during dispatch, matching Xray v26.9.9 `common/mux/server.go` `handleStatusKeep`; failed delivery of Chimera's directional `END|HALF_CLOSE` similarly closes only that logical session. Both paths leave other Mux sessions active. The reader's failure log now includes status, data and half-close bits for future protocol diagnostics. This does not change the existing compatibility boundary: reserved option bit `0x04` remains a Chimera peer extension and is not claimed as Xray half-close interoperability.

`mux_server_keeps_physical_worker_alive_for_closed_logical_routes` injects both stale `KEEP` and directional-close routes, checks their per-session `END` replies, then verifies a following valid TCP `NEW` still reaches its target. The 40-test Mux-filtered unit group passed. Five separate `bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh --tcp-iperf-diagnostic` runs passed with iperf3 3.20, each returning nonzero sent/received bytes in valid JSON. The full path then passed at MTU 1280 with `TUN_GATEWAY_MTU=1280 bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh`; its TCP iperf3 sent 59,899,904 bytes and received 58,064,896 bytes at 154.1 Mbit/s, while UDP iperf3 transferred 375,600 bytes each way with 0% loss. The same run passed IPv4/IPv6 fragment pressure, 4 MiB TCP byte comparison and half-close, three UDP idle-expiry cycles, Hub recovery, three Edge restarts and SIGTERM TUN cleanup. These are controlled namespace functionality checks, not a performance benchmark or physical-LAN claim. `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features --locked -- -D warnings`, `cargo fmt --all -- --check`, `git diff --check` and `bash -n scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh` passed. The site-gateway compatibility status remains Partial.

After this change, `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_xray_hub_netns.sh` also passed with Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)` and the fixed `ref/xray-core` baseline. The isolated path covered IPv4 RAW/TLS and REALITY, XHTTP/TLS H2 and H3 modes, IPv6 WebSocket/TLS, TCP/UDP and 4 KiB UDP, explicit prefix mapping, and the existing wrong-credential/security and ACL denials. This confirms the stale-session handling change did not regress those standard Xray Hub/Portal flows; it does not certify Xray support for Chimera's reserved half-close bit.

### 13.63 Full Reverse site path at the configured jumbo MTU (2026-10-05)

The first full MTU-9000 attempt showed that the Office test host advertised route MTU 9000 while its paired veth remained at the Linux default MTU 1500; a 4 KiB Office-LAN UDP request could not reach the Gateway. This was an inconsistent namespace fixture, not evidence of a server forwarding failure. The smoke now sets and asserts the Office route and both veth endpoints to the requested MTU. `TUN_GATEWAY_MTU=9000 bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh --hub-policy-only` passed the focused dual-stack, 4 KiB UDP and live-policy checks; the full `TUN_GATEWAY_MTU=9000 bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh` then passed TCP/UDP forwarding, TCP/UDP iperf3, 4 KiB Office-LAN UDP, IPv4/IPv6 fragment pressure, three UDP idle-expiry recoveries, Hub reconnect, three Edge restarts, and TUN cleanup. TCP iperf3 sent 69,861,376 bytes and received 67,895,296 bytes at 180.9 Mbit/s; UDP iperf3 transferred 375,600 bytes each way with 0% loss. These are controlled functional probes, not throughput claims. The 9000 test configures the disposable Office host-to-Gateway veth as a jumbo link; physical LAN PMTU, mixed-MTU networks and production routes remain unverified. Site-gateway support remains Partial.

### 13.64 Reap completed Reverse Mux session task handles (2026-10-05)

Both `MuxClientWorker` (Portal) and `MuxServerWorker` (Bridge) previously appended one `JoinHandle` per spawned session task to a worker-owned vector and retained every completed handle until the physical worker closed. A long-lived Reverse link with repeated short sessions could therefore grow bookkeeping with lifetime session count. The shared `session_core::track_task` now removes handles for finished tasks before recording each new TCP or UDP task. Active tasks remain registered for worker shutdown/abort, and the vector's retained session history is bounded by its concurrent high-water mark rather than total session churn. Portal packet sessions remain owned by returned `ReversePacketSession` values and do not spawn JoinHandle tasks.

`completed_task_handles_are_reaped_when_new_tasks_are_tracked` creates 512 sequentially completed tasks and confirms the tracked handle vector stays at one. The repeated Portal→Bridge TCP final-response/half-close test and 40-test Mux-filtered group passed after the change. `cargo check -p chimera_server_app --no-default-features --features vless-reverse --locked`, workspace all-target/all-feature Clippy with warnings denied, formatting, shell and diff checks passed. The fixed Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)` namespace suite `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_xray_hub_netns.sh` passed after the change for RAW/TLS, REALITY, XHTTP/TLS H2/H3 and WebSocket/TLS Hub/Portal paths. This bounds retained handle history, not active session counts or payload memory; site-gateway support remains Partial.

### 13.65 Keep Reverse site ACL targets stable under sniffing (2026-10-05)

The Edge Bridge applies `reverse.siteToSite` prefix translation and mapped-target ACL before `BridgeTcpDispatcher::open_tcp`. That dispatcher may sniff HTTP/TLS and, when `routeOnly` is false, replace the actual dial target with the sniffed hostname. Combining those options could therefore dial an address that never passed the configured site map/allow policy. Reverse endpoint compilation now rejects active `sniffing.destOverride` (`http`/`tls`) with `siteToSite` unless `routeOnly: true`; route-only mode retains the checked mapped IP as the dial target while allowing sniffed-domain route metadata. Configurations without the Chimera site policy retain existing Xray-shaped sniffing behavior.

`static_vless_reverse_site_to_site_rejects_target_override_without_route_only` confirms the bypass-capable combination fails during configuration compilation and the route-only combination remains accepted. It passed under both all features and the reduced `vless-reverse` build. `cargo fmt --all -- --check` and `git diff --check` passed. This is a Chimera extension constraint, not a change to Xray's ordinary Reverse sniffing; the site-gateway compatibility status remains Partial.

### 13.66 Sustained site-gateway TCP and UDP traffic probe (2026-10-06)

The namespace harness adds `--iperf-stress-only` with a configurable 10–3600 second interval. A 60-second, four-stream run passed across the live Linux TUN → Office VLESS → Hub Reverse → Edge `siteToSite` mapping/ACL → LAN path: four parallel TCP streams sent 1,230,635,008 bytes and received 1,222,639,616 bytes; four parallel UDP streams, each configured for 5 Mbit/s with 1200-byte payloads, sent and received 150,004,800 bytes with 0.00% reported loss. The checker requires both directions to cover at least 95% of the requested interval and rejects UDP loss above 1%. Validation used `TUN_GATEWAY_STRESS_SECONDS=60 bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh --iperf-stress-only` (pass), `bash -n scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh` (pass), and an invalid two-second setting (correctly rejected with exit 2). The maximum six-stream setting also passed a 10-second live run: TCP sent 215,875,584 bytes and received 204,734,464; UDP sent/received 37,504,800 bytes with 0% loss. The JSON checker verifies the reported stream count matches the requested count. Environment: Linux kernel 6.18.35, rustc 1.98.0, iperf3 3.20, server package 0.9.1.

This is sustained functional evidence inside disposable namespaces, not a comparable performance benchmark. CPU/RSS samples are recorded separately in 13.67; physical LAN behavior, production routing, longer runs and larger multi-client/session concurrency remain unverified. The site-gateway support status remains Partial.

### 13.67 Sample process CPU and RSS during live TUN stress (2026-10-06)

`--iperf-stress-only` now samples `/proc/<pid>/status` RSS and sums per-thread user/system ticks from `/proc/<pid>/task/*/stat` every 100 ms for the Office Gateway, Hub and Edge processes. A repeat of `TUN_GATEWAY_STRESS_SECONDS=60 TUN_GATEWAY_STRESS_PARALLEL=4 bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh --iperf-stress-only` passed. During the 60-second TCP phase, sampled peak RSS was 58,508 KiB (Gateway), 48,876 KiB (Hub) and 43,936 KiB (Edge); aggregate thread CPU time was +66.62, +8.55 and +6.99 seconds respectively. During the UDP phase, sampled peak RSS was 59,312, 49,308 and 44,976 KiB; aggregate thread CPU time was +18.20, +10.03 and +23.40 seconds. The TCP report sent 1,224,998,912 bytes and received 1,216,610,304. UDP sent 150,004,800 and received 150,001,200 bytes; iperf3 rounded the reported loss to 0.00% (a 3,600-byte difference, below the 1% gate).

These are 100 ms sampled process RSS and aggregate thread-time readings from disposable namespaces with local fixtures. They include the specific server build, allocator, kernel and synthetic route, and do not establish production capacity, physical NIC cost, host-wide memory, or a comparable benchmark. Resource samples are diagnostic evidence only; support remains Partial.

### 13.68 Two independent Office clients under concurrent TUN load (2026-10-06)

The namespace harness now accepts `TUN_GATEWAY_STRESS_CLIENTS=2` and runs independent Office client namespaces against separate mapped Edge targets at the same time. `TUN_GATEWAY_STRESS_SECONDS=10 TUN_GATEWAY_STRESS_CLIENTS=2 TUN_GATEWAY_STRESS_PARALLEL=2 bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh --iperf-stress-only` passed: each host sustained two TCP streams for 10 seconds (aggregate 211,025,920 bytes sent / 203,948,032 received), then two UDP streams per host at 5 Mbit/s per stream with 1200-byte payloads (25,003,200 bytes sent and received, 0.00% reported loss). Per-client JSON checks require the requested streams and at least 95% of the requested duration; each UDP client must report no more than 1% loss. The test also sampled Gateway/Hub/Edge resources during each phase. A 10-second single-host/four-stream regression passed immediately afterward (TCP 210,763,776 / 203,554,816 bytes; UDP 25,003,200 / 25,003,200 bytes, 0.00% loss). `bash -n scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh` passed.

The 10-second case exercises two distinct source namespaces and target mappings with two streams per host. A second run used one stream per host for the full 60 seconds: TCP sent 1,199,439,872 / received 1,194,459,136 bytes; UDP sent/received 75,002,400 bytes at 5 Mbit/s per host with 0.00% reported loss. Sampled Gateway/Hub/Edge RSS peaks were 53,852/46,660/43,116 KiB during TCP and 54,524/46,944/44,316 KiB during UDP. This remains synthetic namespace evidence; it does not establish production throughput, hardware capacity, physical LAN behavior, broader concurrency or stability beyond 60 seconds. Site-gateway support remains Partial.

### 13.69 Reject conflicting IP fragment end lengths (2026-10-06)

The vendored `FragmentReassembler` regression constructs IPv4 and IPv6 sets with a non-final first fragment, then two final fragments declaring different packet end offsets. Both cases reject the conflicting tail and clear all state; valid fragments with the same key subsequently reassemble byte-for-byte. The full vendor suite passed (20 unit, 34 integration), its all-target/all-feature Clippy check passed with warnings denied, and the Server reduced-feature `tun_udp_` suite passed (5 tests), retaining TUN-path truncated/overlap rejection and service recovery coverage. Root and vendor formatting checks and `git diff --check` passed. This does not close other malformed-fragment combinations, sustained live reassembly pressure, physical LAN or production routing; site-gateway support remains Partial.


### 13.70 Conflicting fragment-tail recovery through the TUN service adapter (2026-10-06)

The MemoryTun Server regression `tun_udp_conflicting_fragment_end_lengths_are_dropped_and_recover_for_both_families` verifies IPv4 and IPv6 conflict handling at the service boundary: no UDP payload reaches the Reverse Mux while final fragments disagree on packet length, and a valid fragment set reusing the same identification subsequently reaches the expected target with intact payload. `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --no-default-features --features tun-gateway,vless-reverse --lib tun_udp_ --locked -- --nocapture` passed (6 tests). `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features --locked -- -D warnings`, `cargo fmt --all -- --check`, and `git diff --check` passed. The reduced-feature test build emitted four existing unused-import warnings. This is MemoryTun service-adapter coverage, not live kernel-TUN, Xray TUN interoperability, or physical-network evidence; additional malformed-fragment cases remain open and site-gateway support remains Partial.


### 13.71 Conflicting fragment tails over a live Linux TUN (2026-10-06)

The ordinary Office-host raw-packet probe in `scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh` now injects an IPv4 and IPv6 UDP fragment set whose final fragments declare different packet end offsets. Each malformed set must produce no echo; correct fragments that reuse the same IP fragment ID must then reassemble through the real system-TUN → Reverse → Edge path, return the exact UDP payload, and appear once in the Edge fixture log. The probe reuses the existing fragment-pressure source tuple so it does not alter the configured UDP session-capacity case. The full namespace smoke passed on Linux kernel 6.18.35 / rustc 1.98.0 / iperf3 3.20 / Server 0.9.1, including existing reassembly pressure, TCP/UDP forwarding, idle expiry, restart and TUN teardown checks. `bash -n scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh` and `git diff --check` passed. This is disposable namespace evidence, not physical-LAN, production-capacity, Xray TUN or other malformed-fragment coverage; support remains Partial.


### 13.72 Live TUN TCP and UDP fail closed without an Edge Bridge (2026-10-06)

`bash scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh --reverse-offline-only` now checks the live Office→Linux system TUN→Hub path when its sole Chimera Edge Bridge is stopped. A new TCP attempt returns no payload and does not reach the Edge LAN; UDP receives no response and likewise leaves no Edge marker. After Bridge reattachment, a fresh TCP connection and the same UDP socket/source port/Overlay target both recover. The test passed in three consecutive runs on Linux kernel 6.18.35 with rustc 1.98.0 and Chimera Server 0.9.1; the script builds with `full,tun-gateway`, and `bash -n scripts/test_tun_gateway_netns.sh` passed. Existing `ack_rate` dead-code warnings occur during the app build. This is controlled Chimera-to-Chimera namespace evidence, not physical network partition or Xray TUN interoperability; UDP loss during outages is not ruled out and site-gateway support remains Partial.

### 13.73 Linux Xray TUN inbound and auto-route lifecycle (2026-10-08)

Added the opt-in `xray-tun` capability, sharing Linux device and vendored `watfaq-netstack` runtime dependencies with the separate `tun-gateway` extension. `protocol: "tun"` compiles into a device-backed server plan rather than a socket listener; the static Linux plan applies Xray defaults (`mtu: 1500`, generated `utun10`–`utun1024` name), configures `gateway` interface addresses, carries inbound tag/user level and TCP sniffing into the shared dispatcher, and is supervised by the existing server service-task owner. Local IPv4/IPv6 ICMP Echo replies use the vendored stack behavior. Linux `autoSystemRoutingTable` now installs Xray-shaped main-table routes with metric 1 and removes them during startup rollback and shutdown. Default routes are accepted to match Xray, with an operational warning because they can capture the server's own outbound connections. The Linux Xray baseline ignores `desc`, `dns`, and `autoOutboundsInterface`, so Chimera preserves that behavior. At this stage, QUIC sniffing was rejected; the follow-up route-only SNI slice is recorded below. The shared netstack requires MTU 1280–65535, so invalid values fail during plan compilation before creating resources. Active TCP and UDP sessions are bounded at 512 and 1024. Non-Linux builds and builds without `xray-tun` fail explicitly.

Validation: `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --no-default-features --features xray-tun --lib xray_tun --locked -- --nocapture` passed (9 tests), covering config defaults/validation including MTU boundaries, route construction and metric, disabled capability errors, validated-plan separation from socket inbounds, MemoryTun TCP/UDP echo through the shared Dokodemo dispatcher, and local IPv4/IPv6 ICMP Echo replies. `bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed in a disposable Linux user/network namespace: a live kernel TUN installed IPv4/IPv6 main-table routes with metric 1, an Office-namespace client completed TCP and UDP echo through the TUN/netstack/Dokodemo/Freedom path for both address families, a conflicting second route triggered partial-install rollback while preserving the pre-existing route, and SIGTERM shutdown removed routes, gateway addresses, and the interface. The reduced-feature build emits unrelated existing unused/dead-code warnings. This verifies raw dual-stack TUN traffic against isolated echo targets, not Xray 26.9.9 client/runtime interoperability. This remains partial Linux static TCP/UDP and local Echo support, not a full compatibility claim: QUIC sniffed-domain routing, ICMP errors, a broader TCP/UDP/security matrix, dynamic management, and real Xray-reference traffic comparison remain open; keep the Xray TUN matrix Partial.

Final workspace validation after preserving the existing `tunGateway` startup-error context: `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features --locked` passed (1705 tests), `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features --locked -- -D warnings`, `cargo fmt --all -- --check`, and `git diff --check` passed. The focused live namespace test was rerun and passed after this adjustment.

### 13.74 Route-only QUIC SNI matching for Linux Xray TUN (2026-10-08)

The TUN UDP path honors `sniffing.enabled`, `destOverride: ["quic"]`, and `routeOnly: true` for QUIC v1, v2, and draft-29 Initial packets. It decrypts Initial CRYPTO data and supplies the ClientHello SNI only to routing while preserving the packet's original IP:port as its actual destination. CRYPTO fragments are reassembled across coalesced packets and subsequent UDP datagrams. An incomplete probe buffers at most 8 datagrams / 8 KiB for up to 500 ms; completion pins the SNI route for the UDP idle lifetime, while timeout or resource-cap fallback uses the original IP route. The parser follows Xray's QUIC ECH behavior and uses an available outer SNI even when an ECH extension is present; the separate TCP TLS sniffer remains ECH-conservative. Non-routeOnly QUIC destination rewriting remains an explicit configuration error. This follows pinned Xray behavior in `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`: `proxy/tun/handler.go` passes sniffing to UDP dispatch, and `app/dispatcher/default.go` preserves the original target while using `RouteTarget` for route-only matching; `common/protocol/quic/sniff.go` assembles CRYPTO data and reads SNI from the outer ClientHello.

Validation: parser tests pass for pinned upstream QUIC v1/v2 Initial fixtures, a captured Xray `26.9.9` XHTTP/H3 ClientHello split across two UDP datagrams, malformed/truncated input, varint boundaries, and conflicting CRYPTO fragments. The MemoryTun integration uses those captured Initials to verify the first fragment is held, the second completes `www.google.com` SNI, the domain rule selects `blocked` ahead of direct UDP fallback, and later packets on the same tuple stay blackholed. `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed with Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)` and the pinned `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 server: the client Initial is blocked by the `www.google.com` SNI rule; a separate TLS-trusted XHTTP/H3 `packet-up` VLESS session then completes through the live TUN to the reference server, returning both SOCKS TCP and UDP echo payloads. The script asserts Chimera logged the `site-tls.test` route-only flow, and the ordinary dual-stack TCP/UDP echo, route-conflict rollback, and shutdown cleanup also pass. This verifies one fixed Xray client/server, XHTTP/H3 `packet-up`, route-only SNI, and IPv4 TCP/UDP target combination; other versions, profiles, target families, ICMP errors, and transport/security combinations remain unverified. The support matrix remains Partial.

The current Xray TUN claim is still limited to Linux static forwarding, QUIC v1/v2/draft-29 route-only SNI, and the exact probes above. Full Xray client/server session interoperability is verified only for the single fixed XHTTP/H3 `packet-up` TCP/UDP case; other ICMP error behavior, dynamic management and broader protocol/security/transport combinations remain open.

Final validation for the preceding implementation slice: `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features --locked` passed (1713 tests); `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features --locked -- -D warnings`, `cargo check -p chimera_server_app --no-default-features --features tun-gateway,vless-reverse --locked`, `cargo fmt --all -- --check`, `git diff --check`, and `bash -n scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed. The focused `xray-tun` MemoryTun test passed. The reduced-feature application build has existing unused/dead-code warnings; the all-feature Clippy run is warning-free. The subsequent full pinned-Xray probe and its exact interoperability scope are recorded above.

### 13.75 Full Xray H3 VLESS session through Linux Xray TUN (2026-10-08)

Extended the optional live namespace probe to start the pinned Xray 26.9.9 reference VLESS/XHTTP H3 server and connect to it with a pinned Xray client using a test-trusted certificate. The client uses SOCKS TCP and UDP requests to exercise VLESS TCP and UDP through the XHTTP H3 `packet-up` transport; both echo payloads return through the Chimera TUN. The probe keeps a separate SNI blackhole check and requires the Chimera log to show the `site-tls.test` route-only UDP flow. Its reference Freedom outbound explicitly allows only the fixture IP (`198.18.44.20/32`), so this test exception does not broaden the production Chimera server policy. The existing live IPv4/IPv6 TCP/UDP TUN echo and lifecycle checks also pass. This verifies one Linux/Xray 26.9.9 client-server/profile combination only; support remains Partial and ICMP errors, dynamic TUN management, other address/protocol/security/transport combinations, and broader client versions remain open.

### 13.76 Unknown IP protocol errors through Linux Xray TUN (2026-10-08)

Unknown IP protocol numbers were previously dropped in the vendored packet classifier before the IP stack could produce an error. The classifier now forwards unrecognized IPv4 protocol and IPv6 Next Header values through the stack path; when the TCP listener finds no parsed transport, it reinjects the packet into the IP device so smoltcp can generate the protocol error. Known unsupported IPv6 extension headers remain explicitly dropped. This matches the pinned Xray TUN behavior for the tested unknown value 252: IPv4 returns ICMP Destination Unreachable/Protocol Unreachable (type 3/code 2), while IPv6 returns ICMPv6 Parameter Problem/Unrecognized Next Header (type 4/code 1). Other ICMP errors, PMTU behavior, and extension-header handling are not covered by this slice.

The baseline is `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 at commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`; the comparison binary reports `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`. `vendor/watfaq-netstack/tests/integration_test.rs::unknown_ip_protocols_return_family_specific_icmp_errors` checks both response types and reversed address tuples. The Xray TUN MemoryTun test covers the same behavior at the server adapter, and `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` injects raw IPv4/IPv6 protocol-252 packets through the live Linux TUN and checks the corresponding errors from both Chimera and pinned Xray. Vendor and server focused tests plus the live namespace probe passed. This closes only unknown-protocol error generation; broader ICMP and Xray TUN compatibility remain Partial.

### 13.77 Linux Xray TUN PMTU errors follow the device MTU (2026-10-08)

The configured MTU is applied to the Linux TUN interface and to the user-space packet stack. For routed Office traffic whose IP packet exceeds that interface MTU, Linux's forwarding route produces the PMTU response before the packet reaches the TUN reader; the server does not need a second user-space ICMP generator. This matches the pinned Xray Linux implementation: `proxy/tun/tun_linux.go::setup` sets the link MTU and `newEndpoint` passes the same MTU to gVisor. Chimera's `start_xray_tun_server` likewise configures the device MTU and passes it to `NetStack::new_with_mtu`.

The live namespace probe uses a 1400-byte TUN, sends 1500-byte IPv4 UDP with DF and 1500-byte IPv6 UDP through the routed Office link, then verifies the quoted source/destination tuple and that IPv4 Fragmentation Needed (type 3/code 4) and IPv6 Packet Too Big (type 2/code 0) both advertise MTU 1400. `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed for Chimera and Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`, using `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`; shell syntax and diff checks passed. This verifies Linux routed-TUN PMTU signaling at MTU 1400 in isolated namespaces. Other configured MTUs, PMTU changes learned from downstream networks, ICMP filtering, and physical-network behavior remain unverified; Xray TUN support remains Partial.

### 13.78 Downstream UDP send errors are not converted into TUN ICMP (2026-10-08)

The pinned Xray baseline is `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 at commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. Its `proxy/tun/stack_gvisor_icmp_handler.go::handleICMPEchoPacket` consumes non-Echo ICMP without generating a TUN response. Its UDP adapter in `proxy/tun/udp_fullcone.go` carries UDP payloads through `writePacket`; it does not translate an outbound UDP socket error into a client-facing ICMP Fragmentation Needed or Packet Too Big packet. Chimera's `run_freedom_udp_session` similarly logs an outbound `send_to` failure and ends that UDP flow without constructing or injecting an ICMP response. No runtime code change is needed to preserve this observed Xray boundary.

The live namespace probe now sends 1400-byte IPv4/IPv6 UDP packets through each TUN to a downstream fixture. For every received datagram, the fixture emits the matching IPv4 Fragmentation Needed or IPv6 Packet Too Big message with MTU 1280 and a quote of the server's outbound UDP tuple. An `AF_PACKET` capture on the server's egress veth confirms both ICMP families reach the server namespace. Chimera and pinned Xray 26.9.9 return neither a UDP payload nor a matching PTB to the TUN client. Chimera's session log shows no outbound UDP send/receive error for these probes. A direct unconnected UDP control also sees no `POLLERR` and no error on a later send when `IP_RECVERR`/`IPV6_RECVERR` is disabled; enabling those options lets `recvmsg(MSG_ERRQUEUE)` report the expected ICMP type/code and MTU 1280 for both families. This matches Linux's opt-in error-queue contract ([`udp(7)`](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man7/udp.7.html), [`IP_RECVERR`](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/IP_RECVERR.2const.html), [`IPV6_RECVERR`](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/IPV6_RECVERR.2const.html)). The pinned Xray TUN adapter does not enable or consume that queue and does not translate those errors into client-facing ICMP. `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passes with Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)` and baseline `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. This verifies the tested PTB-to-egress-interface/no-client-PTB behavior and direct Linux error-queue control, not general PMTU learning, other MTUs, or physical-network behavior. The Xray TUN matrix remains Partial.

### 13.79 Non-Echo ICMP Destination Unreachable is consumed (2026-10-08)

The pinned Xray TUN ICMP handlers answer Echo Requests and consume other ICMP messages without sending a packet back. The Xray TUN MemoryTun regression injects valid IPv4 Destination Unreachable (type 3/code 3) and IPv6 Destination Unreachable (type 1/code 4) packets carrying quoted UDP, and asserts that neither produces TUN output. The live namespace probe repeats both error inputs against Chimera and pinned Xray 26.9.9; both consume them without a reply, while the existing unknown-protocol and PMTU probes still pass. `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed with client/server `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)` and baseline `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. The following iteration extends this evidence to Time Exceeded and Parameter Problem; other codes and malformed ICMP remain unverified. The Xray TUN matrix remains Partial.

### 13.80 Time Exceeded and Parameter Problem are consumed by Linux Xray TUN (2026-10-08)


The Xray TUN MemoryTun regression now also injects IPv4 Time Exceeded (type 11/code 0), IPv4 Parameter Problem (type 12/code 0), IPv6 Time Exceeded (type 3/code 0), and IPv6 Parameter Problem (type 4/code 0), each with a quoted UDP packet, and asserts no TUN output after each. The Linux namespace probe injects these four packets through both the Chimera TUN and pinned Xray 26.9.9 TUN; all are consumed without a reply. Existing IPv4/IPv6 Destination Unreachable, unknown-protocol, local-MTU PMTU, downstream PTB, dual-stack TCP/UDP and XHTTP/H3 interoperability checks also pass. `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed with Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)` and baseline `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. The test covers only the stated ICMP types/codes on Linux; alternate codes, malformed/truncated ICMP, extension-header interactions and other platforms remain unverified. Xray TUN support remains Partial.

### 13.81 Truncated ICMP Echo Requests are silently dropped by Linux Xray TUN (2026-10-08)

The Xray TUN MemoryTun regression and the live namespace probe now inject IPv4 and IPv6 Echo Requests containing only the four-byte type/code/checksum prefix, with valid outer IP headers. Neither Chimera nor pinned Xray 26.9.9 emits a TUN reply. This matches the reference `proxy/tun/stack_gvisor_icmp_handler.go::handleICMPEchoPacket`, which consumes ICMP packets that cannot be parsed as Echo Requests. No runtime change was required.

Validation: `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features xray_tun_stack_matches_echo_and_unknown_protocol_icmp_behavior` passed (1 test). `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed for Chimera and Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`, using baseline `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`; the probe also retained its existing dual-stack TCP/UDP, QUIC, PTB, XHTTP/H3 and shutdown checks. This verifies one four-byte truncated Echo form for each IP family on Linux; other malformed ICMP lengths, invalid checksums, extension-header interactions and other platforms remain unverified. Xray TUN support remains Partial.

### 13.82 Xray TUN IPv4 Echo replies tolerate an invalid request checksum (2026-10-08)

The pinned Xray v26.9.9 `proxy/tun/icmp/packet.go::ParseEchoRequest` validates ICMP message length, type and code but does not verify an ICMPv4 checksum; the live reference TUN therefore answers a complete IPv4 Echo Request with an invalid ICMP checksum and recomputes a valid reply checksum. The same probe confirms a bad ICMPv6 checksum is dropped by Xray's IPv6 stack path. Chimera's shared smoltcp device rejects the bad IPv4 checksum before its Echo handler, so the Xray TUN adapter now repairs only an invalid, unfragmented IPv4 Echo Request checksum immediately before stack input. Valid packets, other ICMP types, fragments, IPv6 and the separate Chimera `tun-gateway` forwarding plan are unchanged.

The MemoryTun regression verifies that an invalid-checksum IPv4 Echo Request receives an Echo Reply with the original payload, while invalid-checksum IPv6 Echo still produces no response. The live probe verifies the same behavior against Chimera and Xray 26.9.9, including the IPv4 reply checksum; existing valid Echo, truncated-message, ICMP-error, PMTU, dual-stack forwarding, QUIC and XHTTP/H3 checks pass. Validation used `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features xray_tun_stack_matches_echo_and_unknown_protocol_icmp_behavior` and `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` with `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. This is Linux evidence for complete unfragmented Echo messages only; malformed lengths, fragmented Echo checksum handling, extension-header interactions and other platforms remain unverified. Xray TUN support remains Partial.

### 13.83 Fragmented IPv4 Echo checksum behavior matches pinned Xray (2026-10-08)

The checksum compatibility normalization now runs in the vendored TCP/IP packet pump after IP fragment reassembly and before smoltcp device injection. `NetStack::new_with_mtu_accepting_invalid_ipv4_icmp_echo_checksum` opts Xray TUN into this behavior; existing constructors remain strict, so Chimera's separate `tun-gateway` path does not change. The policy repairs only a complete IPv4 Echo Request with a bad ICMP checksum. Other ICMPv4 messages and IPv6 remain governed by the stack's normal validation.

The vendor integration regressions send IPv4 fragments out of order, verify default mode drops the bad checksum, verify Xray-compatible mode replies with a correct checksum and unchanged reassembled payload, and verify the opt-in mode still drops a fragmented IPv6 Echo with an invalid checksum. The namespace probe sends the same malformed IPv4/IPv6 cases to Chimera and pinned Xray 26.9.9; both answer IPv4 and drop IPv6. For this odd-length raw-socket IPv4 fixture, both live Linux TUN paths return the same trailing zero byte, while direct in-memory netstack tests preserve the reassembled payload byte-for-byte. This observed fixture detail is recorded so future changes compare equivalent device-path behavior rather than assuming the in-memory adapter includes Linux packet framing effects.

Validation: `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml --test integration_test fragmented_ipv4_icmp -- --nocapture` passed (2 tests); `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features xray_tun_stack_matches_echo_and_unknown_protocol_icmp_behavior` passed (1 test); `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed for Chimera and `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`, using `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. This closes only the tested out-of-order two-fragment Echo checksum behavior on Linux; other fragment shapes, malformed lengths, extension-header interactions, and other platforms remain unverified. Xray TUN support remains Partial.

### 13.84 Fragmented IPv6 Echo behind a padding-only Hop-by-Hop header (2026-10-08)

The IPv6 fragment reassembler accepts a padding-only Hop-by-Hop Options header before the Fragment header and removes it from the reconstructed packet before transport processing. The live namespace probe sends the same shape through the Chimera Xray TUN and pinned Xray 26.9.9 TUN; both return the original Echo payload with a valid ICMPv6 checksum. The reduced `xray-tun` build reports existing unused/dead-code warnings.

### 13.85 Padding-only Destination Options after IPv6 Fragment (2026-10-08)

The existing vendored IPv6 fragment reassembler parses padding-only Destination Options in the reassembled fragmentable payload (`IPv6 → Fragment → Destination Options → ICMPv6`). No runtime behavior changed. A new regression places an eight-byte Destination Options header (PadN only) at the start of the fragmentable payload, then sends the second fragment before the first. The test asserts the ICMPv6 Echo Reply identifier, sequence, unchanged application payload, and valid IPv6 pseudo-header checksum. The live Linux TUN probe now tests both this ordering and the previously covered `IPv6 → Hop-by-Hop → Fragment → ICMPv6` ordering independently, using distinct Echo sequence/payload markers. Chimera and pinned Xray 26.9.9 both return a checksum-valid Echo Reply with the original payload for the Destination Options case.

Validation: `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml --test integration_test fragmented_ipv6_icmp_echo_with_padding_destination_options_reassembles -- --exact --nocapture` passed (1 test); `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed against pinned Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)` at `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`, including the previous dual-stack routing, QUIC SNI, XHTTP/H3, PMTU, ICMP and cleanup probes. This verifies only padding-only Destination Options immediately after Fragment with a two-fragment out-of-order ICMPv6 Echo on Linux; semantic options, multiple extension headers and alternative ordering combinations remain unverified. Xray TUN support stays Partial.

### 13.86 Unknown IPv6 Destination Option action bits after Fragment (2026-10-08)

A new live Linux TUN comparison found a real compatibility gap in the `IPv6 → Fragment → Destination Options → ICMPv6 Echo` path. All four unknown-option actions were previously dropped by Chimera's strict fragment parser. Pinned Xray 26.9.9 at `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120` handles the four representative unknown option types as follows: `0x1e` (action 00) skips and returns Echo Reply; `0x5e` (action 01) silently discards; `0x9e` (action 10) and `0xde` (action 11, unicast destination) send ICMPv6 Parameter Problem code 2 with pointer 42. Each probe injects two out-of-order fragments with an eight-byte Destination Options header in the fragmentable portion and checks response source, Echo payload/checksum or Parameter Problem type/code/pointer, quoted offending option and checksum.

Chimera now opts the Xray TUN netstack constructor into RFC 8200 unknown-option action processing only after IPv6 fragment reassembly. For action 00 the unknown option is skipped; for 01 the fragment set is discarded; for 10/11 a bounded ICMPv6 Parameter Problem response quotes the reassembled IPv6 packet, with the error pointer indexing the offending option in that packet. Action 11 suppresses the error for multicast. The output uses nonblocking bounded queue admission so an error reply cannot stall packet ingestion. This is a separate opt-in from the prior invalid IPv4 Echo checksum option; the `tun-gateway` default constructor retains strict rejection of all non-padding options. Recognized semantic IPv6 options are not fully implemented, and behavior outside the tested unicast two-fragment ICMPv6 path remains unverified; the feature remains Partial.

Verification: `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml --test integration_test fragmented_ipv6_unknown_destination_option_actions_are_xray_tun_only -- --exact --nocapture` passed, including strict-mode negative cases, opt-in responses, quoted data and checksum. `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed after the fix with `Xray 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`, verifying four matching outcomes on both live TUNs while retaining QUIC route-only, XHTTP/H3 TCP/UDP, MTU/ICMP, route rollback and shutdown coverage. The other extension-header placements, named/recognized semantic options, multicast behavior, malformed lengths and arbitrary fragmentation layouts require independent verification.

### 13.87 Fragmented IPv6 Destination Options length overrun and zero-length boundaries (2026-10-08)

The pinned Xray TUN reference is `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`, using local Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`. The live Linux namespace probe now sends out-of-order two-fragment `IPv6 → Fragment → Destination Options → ICMPv6 Echo` packets with a complete eight-byte Destination Options header. Two malformed option lengths claim five data bytes even though only four bytes remain in the header: an unknown skip-action option (`0x1e`) and `PadN` (type 1). Both Chimera and pinned Xray silently drop both malformed packets without Echo Reply or matching Parameter Problem. A third packet uses the same unknown option type `0x1e` with a valid zero-length body and trailing Pad1 bytes; both implementations skip the option and return a checksum-valid Echo Reply preserving its identifier, sequence, and payload.

Existing `xray-tun` parsing already matches those three outcomes, so there is no runtime change. A new vendored netstack regression tests all three cases with out-of-order fragments in both strict-default and Xray opt-in constructors: strict mode rejects unknown option types, while Xray opt-in mode discards both malformed lengths but accepts the valid zero-length unknown option. After each malformed case the same stack accepts a valid two-fragment packet with the same fragment identification, checking reassembly state is not poisoned; reply identifier, sequence, payload and ICMPv6 pseudo-header checksum are checked.

Validation: `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml --test integration_test fragmented_ipv6_destination_option_length_boundaries_match_xray -- --exact --nocapture` passed. `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed with the observational cases, retaining its QUIC route-only, H3 XHTTP TCP/UDP, PMTU, dual-stack forwarding and cleanup checks. The script now asserts the observed outcomes rather than merely printing them. These checks cover only option-body lengths within an otherwise complete eight-byte header on Linux; invalid Hdr Ext Len, truncated fragment headers, other option lengths/ordering, multicast and named semantic options remain unverified. Xray TUN support remains Partial.

### 13.88 IPv6 Destination Options Hdr Ext Len versus first-fragment header completeness (2026-10-08)

A live Linux TUN comparison against pinned Xray 26.9.9 (source `ref/xray-core` at `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`) found a narrow compatibility gap in `IPv6 → Fragment → Destination Options → ICMPv6 Echo`. In an out-of-order two-fragment packet whose first fragment contains 16 bytes of fragmentable data, a Destination Options `Hdr Ext Len` of 1 claims a 16-byte extension despite the fixture physically placing only eight option bytes before a valid ICMPv6 Echo header. Chimera initially misinterpreted Echo type `0x80` as an unknown action-10 option and emitted ICMPv6 Parameter Problem code 2 / pointer 48; pinned Xray silently dropped the packet. Reordering the probe ahead of other ICMP cases reproduced that difference, excluding a previous-response-rate explanation. Two other malformed variants already matched Xray: a claimed 32-byte header with only 17 fragmentable bytes was dropped, and a claimed eight-byte header followed by another eight bytes of padding before ICMP was dropped.

A valid positive control isolates first-fragment header-chain completeness rather than a broad suppression of unknown option errors: a real 16-byte Destination Options header with an unknown action-10 option in its second eight bytes, followed by a complete ICMPv6 Echo header **within fragment zero** (24 fragmentable bytes in that first fragment), produces Parameter Problem code 2 / pointer 48 in both implementations. The same header shape with only 16 first-fragment bytes is dropped by pinned Xray. The small, opt-in Xray TUN fix therefore validates that an IPv6 first fragment with `Fragment.next_header = Destination Options` contains the declared options header and, when its next header is ICMPv6, the following complete eight-byte ICMPv6 header. Incomplete first-fragment header chains are dropped and pending reassembly state is removed. Ordinary `tun-gateway` and default netstack constructors retain their prior behavior. This replaces a provisional checksum-based malformed-header heuristic; it is a header-length boundary, not generic ICMPv6 permissiveness.

Verification: `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml --test integration_test fragmented_ipv6_destination_hdr_ext_len_mismatch_is_dropped -- --exact --nocapture` passes for both default and Xray constructors, all three malformed scenarios, the valid 24-byte control, recovery on a reused fragment ID, and the valid Parameter Problem quote/checksum. `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passes against the pinned Xray 26.9.9 binary; the live test asserts all four outcomes separately on Chimera and Xray and retains TCP/UDP, QUIC, XHTTP/H3, PMTU, route rollback and shutdown checks. This is Linux evidence for the specified Destination Options → ICMPv6 chain and first-fragment layouts only. Other extension-header sequences, upper-layer protocols, truncated fragments and broader RFC 7112 conformance remain unverified; Xray TUN stays Partial.

### 13.89 Chained Destination Options after Fragment and first-fragment completeness (2026-10-08)

A live Linux user/network namespace comparison against pinned Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)` (`ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`) exercises `IPv6 → Fragment → Destination Options → Destination Options → ICMPv6 Echo` with two out-of-order fragments. For two padding-only eight-byte Destination Options headers and a complete eight-byte ICMPv6 header in fragment zero (24 fragmentable bytes), both Chimera and Xray return an Echo Reply preserving the identifier, sequence, payload and checksum. With the *same* header chain but only 16 fragmentable bytes in fragment zero (the ICMPv6 header arrives entirely in the second fragment), Chimera initially returned Echo while Xray silently discarded. A positive control puts an unknown action-10 option (`0x9e`) in the second Destination Options header with the full ICMPv6 header in fragment zero; both return ICMPv6 Parameter Problem code 2 / pointer 50 and a valid checksum, proving this behavior must not be disabled by a broad two-header rejection.

The bounded, opt-in Xray TUN first-fragment guard now walks consecutive Destination Options headers in fragment-zero payload and requires a complete eight-byte ICMPv6 header after their declared lengths. Each extension consumes at least eight bytes, bounding the scan to the first-fragment length without dynamic allocation or a new packet owner. If the declared chain or ICMPv6 header does not fit, the reassembly state is removed and the packet is dropped. This does not change the default netstack / Chimera-only `tun-gateway` behavior, or apply a general RFC 7112 rule to TCP, UDP or other IPv6 extension types. Existing transport-stage unknown-option actions still handle the second header and return pointer 50 when appropriate. The focused regression verifies incomplete-chain dropping, same-fragment-ID recovery, complete-chain Echo and action-10 Parameter Problem including quote/checksum.

The live script now asserts the three comparison outcomes on both TUN implementations. An initial live run completed these new checks but then hit an unrelated pre-existing non-Echo ICMP negative probe: its raw IPv6 socket misclassified an ICMPv6 Neighbor Solicitation (type 135) as a response to Time Exceeded. That probe now filters unrelated sources and informational/neighbor-discovery ICMPv6 types rather than treating them as error replies. The full `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` then passed, including prior dual-stack, QUIC, XHTTP/H3, PMTU, route rollback and shutdown checks. `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed (20 unit tests, 42 integration tests); `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed (40 tests); vendor all-target/all-feature Clippy and workspace all-target/all-feature Clippy with `-D warnings`, Rust formatting, Shell syntax, `git diff --check`, and the reduced `xray-tun` feature build all passed (the reduced build retains existing unused/dead-code warnings). Validation and the remaining compatibility limits are scoped to this exact two-fragment Destination Options → ICMPv6 chain; support stays Partial.

### 13.90 Fragmented IPv6 UDP behind Destination Options: first-fragment transport header (2026-10-08)

The pinned Xray reference remains `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`, with Linux binary `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`. A live Linux TUN comparison uncovered a UDP counterpart to the previously verified ICMPv6 first-fragment boundary. Four out-of-order two-fragment cases use `IPv6 → Fragment → Destination Options → UDP` or `IPv6 → Fragment → Destination Options → Destination Options → UDP`, with padding-only eight-byte options headers and correctly checksummed UDP to the existing test echo port 39644. If fragment zero contains only the extension header(s)—8 or 16 fragmentable bytes respectively—and the UDP header appears entirely in the second fragment, Chimera initially reassembled and forwarded the UDP datagram, while pinned Xray 26.9.9 silently discarded it. When fragment zero contains the complete eight-byte UDP header following the options—16 or 24 fragmentable bytes respectively—both endpoints correctly echo the original UDP payload with valid checksum and port tuple.

The bounded first-fragment options-chain scanner now checks for either an eight-byte ICMPv6 header **or** an eight-byte UDP header in fragment zero. The UDP reader owns its own fragment reassembler and previously did not enable the Xray first-fragment validation; the Xray TUN stack constructor now explicitly sets a distinct `xray_first_fragment_header_guard` on the UDP reader. This opt-in only enforces the observed Destination Options / UDP first-fragment bound and deliberately does *not* enable the TCP/ICMP parser's unknown-option action / ICMP error generation for UDP. Non-Xray netstack and site `tun-gateway` constructors preserve their original behavior. Malformed first fragments remove reassembly state so a later valid fragment set can reuse the same identification.

A focused vendored test `fragmented_ipv6_udp_destination_options_first_fragment_header_is_xray_only` verifies the one/two options variants in both default and opt-in modes, same-ID recovery, UDP payload and ports. The live `run_fragmented_ipv6_udp_destination_first_header_probe` compares and asserts all four cases on Chimera and pinned Xray. Verification: `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed, including all four UDP first-fragment outcomes against both live TUN implementations, XHTTP/H3 and QUIC, PMTU, dual-stack routing and shutdown cleanup. `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed (20 unit, 43 integration); `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed (40); both vendor and workspace all-target/all-feature Clippy with `-D warnings`, Rust formatting, Shell syntax, `git diff --check` and the reduced `xray-tun` app build passed (the reduced build retains existing unused/dead-code warnings). This remains limited to the tested padding-only Destination Options → UDP chain, Linux first fragments and two-fragment layouts; unknown semantic option behavior on UDP, TCP first-fragment completeness, arbitrary extension header chains and broader RFC 7112 conformance remain Partial.

### 13.91 Fragmented IPv6 TCP behind Destination Options: first-fragment minimum (2026-10-09)

The pinned Xray TUN baseline remains `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`, binary `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)` on Linux. A live namespace comparison sent out-of-order two-fragment `IPv6 → Fragment → Destination Options → TCP SYN` traffic, using either one or two padding-only eight-byte Destination Options headers and a TCP SYN with a 24-byte TCP header including an MSS option, followed by a short payload. Fragment zero carried 0, 8, 16 or 24 TCP bytes after the options header chain. In the first trial, Chimera returned SYN-ACK even with zero TCP bytes in fragment zero, while pinned Xray silently dropped that case. More importantly, **both implementations accept TCP when fragment zero contains just eight TCP bytes**, even though a complete 20-byte fixed TCP header or 24-byte header with MSS option does not fit in that first fragment. The 16-byte and complete-header TCP first-fragment variants also return SYN-ACK on both; this observed Xray minimum must not be mistaken for full RFC 7112 TCP header-chain enforcement.

The Xray-TUN-only first-fragment scanner now requires at least eight TCP bytes following one or more declared Destination Options headers in fragment zero, in addition to the previously checked eight-byte ICMPv6/UDP boundaries. The TCP/ICMP packet pump already opts into the scanner, so only the bounded fragment parser in `vendor/watfaq-netstack/src/fragment.rs` changes. No default `tun-gateway` behavior, UDP semantics, application sessions or configuration changes are involved. The vendored `fragmented_ipv6_tcp_destination_options_requires_eight_bytes_in_first_fragment_for_xray` regression checks both options-chain lengths, default-mode compatibility, Xray drop for zero TCP bytes, SYN-ACK with eight TCP bytes and recovery after a rejected fragment using the same ID.

The live `run_fragmented_ipv6_tcp_destination_first_header_probe` exercises all eight cases in each TUN and validates matching SYN-ACK flags/acknowledgment/checksum or silent drop, while keeping existing namespace dual-stack routing, QUIC/H3, ICMP, PMTU, rollback and shutdown probes. Verification: `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed against both live TUNs, including all 16 observations and prior networking/cleanup cases; `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed (20 unit, 44 integration), and `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed (40). Vendor and workspace all-target/all-feature Clippy with `-D warnings`, Rust formatting, Shell syntax, `git diff --check`, and reduced `xray-tun` app build also passed; the reduced build retains its existing unused/dead-code warnings. Unverified: TCP extension options and fragmented data with other TCP header layouts, additional extension types, and broad RFC 7112 behavior; Xray TUN remains Partial.

### 13.92 Fragmented TCP header length and option-boundary matrix (2026-10-09)

An extended live Linux TUN probe compared pinned `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 (`52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`; binary `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`) against Chimera for out-of-order `IPv6 → Fragment → [one/two padding-only Destination Options] → TCP SYN` packets. Three checksum-valid TCP header shapes were tested: 20-byte fixed header with no options; 24-byte TCP header with a four-byte MSS option; and maximum 40-byte TCP header with MSS followed by 16 one-byte NOP options. The first fragment ended at 0, 8, 16 or 24 TCP bytes for the first two header shapes, and also at 32 or 40 bytes for the 40-byte shape. Each fragmented SYN carried a short payload, a unique source port and identification, and a valid IPv6 TCP pseudo-header checksum; responses were checked for SYN-ACK flags, ACK number, TCP checksum and matching ports. All cases ran on both implementations, with one and two eight-byte Destination Options headers: **zero TCP bytes in fragment zero silently drop; eight or more TCP bytes return SYN-ACK**. In particular, dividing a TCP option field across fragments did not change this result. The complete live `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` also passed its original dual-stack forwarding, QUIC/H3, ICMP, PMTU, route rollback and shutdown checks.

Because the existing Xray-only fragment-zero guard already matched every case, **no runtime/parser change was needed**. The live script now includes the option-length matrix as checked cases, and a new vendored regression `fragmented_ipv6_tcp_option_lengths_preserve_xray_first_fragment_boundary` generates real 20/24/40-byte TCP headers with correct checksums, sends out-of-order fragments at the same header boundaries, compares the strict-default and Xray-opt-in constructors, verifies SYN-ACK where accepted, and verifies that a valid fragment set with the same fragment ID recovers after a rejected zero-TCP-byte first fragment. Validation: `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed on both live TUNs with the enlarged TCP option matrix and all existing dual-stack, route rollback and shutdown probes; the focused new vendored regression passed. `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed (20 unit, 45 integration); `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed (40). Vendor and workspace all-target/all-feature Clippy with `-D warnings`, Rust formatting, Shell syntax, `git diff --check`, and the reduced `xray-tun` feature build passed; the reduced build retains its existing warnings. This extends only the measured well-formed TCP SYN/MSS/NOP options and one/two padding-only IPv6 Destination Options layouts; malformed TCP option lengths, other TCP flags, alternative extension headers, resource pressure, and complete RFC 7112 behavior remain unverified. Xray TUN continues to be **Partial**.

### 13.93 Fragmented IPv6 SYN malformed TCP option length acceptance (2026-10-09)

The pinned Linux TUN oracle remains Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)` with source `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. A live out-of-order two-fragment `IPv6 → Fragment → [one/two padding-only Destination Options] → TCP SYN` probe covered eight 24-byte SYN option fields (four-byte MSS option or other four-byte layouts), with fragment zero containing the first 8 or all 24 TCP bytes. TCP checksums and destination ports were valid; every case used an identifiable source port and fragment ID. Valid MSS and EOL-with-trailing-padding controls return SYN-ACK in both implementations. Before this change, Chimera's smoltcp path dropped malformed MSS lengths (0, 3, and a claimed 5 beyond the four-byte options), window-scale length 2, and an unrecognized option with zero length. Pinned Xray accepted these SYNs and returned a checksum-valid SYN-ACK, including when the option bytes were entirely in the second fragment. A malformed truncated Timestamp option also returned SYN-ACK in both implementations. Importantly, an early trial with no TCP data after a complete 24-byte header resulted in an empty final IPv6 fragment, so its drops were discarded as invalid test evidence; the final fixture includes actual TCP payload in the second fragment.

The pinned gVisor `pkg/tcpip/header/tcp.go` `ParseSynOptions` stops parsing SYN options at a malformed known/unknown option, retaining any valid preceding options rather than rejecting the SYN. Chimera's smoltcp rejects several of these cases. To preserve the Xray TUN wire contract without loosening other paths, `vendor/watfaq-netstack/src/tcp_listener.rs` now performs a **bounded opt-in compatibility normalization only after a fragmented IPv6 SYN has been successfully reassembled** in the Xray TUN TCP packet pump. It checks the original IPv6/TCP sizes, SYN flag, TCP header data offset and **original pseudo-header checksum**; only when an option kind/length is malformed does it replace that option and the remaining options with inert NOPs, then recompute the TCP checksum. Valid options before the failure remain intact. This preserves gVisor's stop-at-first-error behavior without introducing a permissive parser into default `tun-gateway`, plain/unfragmented packets or other protocols. It does not establish full TCP option negotiation parity, and no malformed packet payload is logged.

The focused vendored regression `fragmented_ipv6_tcp_malformed_syn_options_follow_xray_without_bypassing_checksum` checks all eight option layouts with one/two Destination Options headers and 8/24 first-fragment TCP bytes, strict-default isolation for the five previously rejected layouts, bad checksum refusal, and same-fragment-ID recovery after an invalid checksum. The live `run_fragmented_ipv6_tcp_malformed_options_probe` compares and asserts the 32 cases on each of Chimera and pinned Xray; `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed, retaining existing dual-stack forwarding, QUIC/H3, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and shutdown checks. `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed (20 unit, 46 integration), and `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed (40). Vendor and workspace all-target/all-feature Clippy with `-D warnings`, Rust formatting, Shell syntax, `git diff --check` and reduced-feature `xray-tun` app build passed (the latter retains previously known warnings). Broader malformed TCP data offsets, non-SYN segments, other extension-header combinations and production workloads remain unverified, so the Xray TUN matrix remains **Partial**.

### 13.94 Fragmented IPv6 TCP SYN Data Offset limits (2026-10-09)

The pinned Xray TUN reference is `ref/xray-core` v26.9.9 commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120` (Linux binary `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`). Out-of-order two-fragment `IPv6 → Fragment → [one/two padding-only Destination Options] → TCP SYN` probes tested the four-bit TCP Data Offset values **0, 4, 5, 6 and 15** with original checksum-correct TCP segments and a nonempty payload. Fragment zero contained either eight TCP bytes or 24 TCP bytes; both implementations were run against the same fixture, which uses four NOP bytes at the beginning of the TCP payload to make Data Offset 6 valid. Xray and Chimera both silently drop Data Offset 0 or 4 (below the 20-byte TCP minimum) and 15 (declares a 60-byte header longer than the actual segment), and both return a checksum-valid SYN-ACK with the expected acknowledgment and port tuple for offsets 5 (normal fixed header) and 6 (valid four-byte NOP option area). This confirms that the earlier Xray-only malformed SYN **option-length** normalization must not be generalized to permit malformed TCP **Data Offset** values.

The existing `xray-tun` packet path already rejects these invalid offsets, so no runtime/parser modification is needed. The live `run_fragmented_ipv6_tcp_data_offset_probe` adds 20 checked input cases per TUN and requires the measured drop or SYN-ACK outcome; a dedicated vendored `fragmented_ipv6_tcp_data_offset_boundaries_match_pinned_xray` integration test covers all 5 × 2 × 2 combinations in both the default netstack and opt-in Xray constructor. The complete `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed all 20 Data Offset cases for each live TUN alongside its existing dual-stack, XHTTP/H3, PMTU, route rollback and shutdown probes. `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed (20 unit, 47 integration); `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed (40). Vendor and workspace all-target/all-feature Clippy with `-D warnings`, Rust formatting, Shell syntax, `git diff --check`, and reduced-feature `xray-tun` app build passed (with its existing warnings). More exotic offsets within larger packets, truncated fixed TCP headers and non-SYN flags are not covered; this is not a general TCP/RFC 7112 parity claim and Xray TUN remains **Partial**.

### 13.95 Fragmented IPv6 non-SYN TCP malformed options and unmatched-flow RST (2026-10-09)

The pinned reference remains Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`, `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. A live Linux network-namespace probe sends out-of-order `IPv6 → Fragment → [one/two padding-only Destination Options] → TCP` packets with **ACK**, **FIN+ACK**, and **RST** flags. All packets use checksummed 24-byte TCP headers (four-byte NOP control, zero-length MSS option, or zero-length unknown option), a nonempty payload, a unique source port/fragment ID and eight or 24 TCP bytes in fragment zero. The synthetic packets have **no established TCP session**. On the pinned Xray TUN, both ACK and FIN+ACK always elicit a checksum-valid **bare RST**, with the response sequence number equal to the original ACK number, whether the TCP options are valid or malformed. An inbound RST elicits no reply. Chimera initially matched the valid options but silently dropped ACK and FIN+ACK with malformed MSS or unknown-option lengths, because the smoltcp packet decoder rejected their TCP options before the unmatched-flow RST could be generated.

The prior Xray-only fragment-reassembly TCP option compatibility normalizer already checked original packet length, header data offset, SYN flags and original IPv6 pseudo-header checksum before replacing malformed options with inert NOP bytes. Its **flag allowlist is now extended only to pure ACK (`0x10`) and FIN+ACK (`0x11`)**, retaining existing SYN handling; input RST and all other unverified flag combinations are untouched. Rewriting is only after successful IPv6 fragment reassembly inside the opt-in Xray TUN TCP packet pump; non-Xray netstack and Chimera-only site `tun-gateway` remain strict. This is an observable no-established-connection RST compatibility result, **not** validation of FIN/ACK in established streams or malformed options inside an active connection.

The live `run_fragmented_ipv6_tcp_non_syn_option_probe` asserts the reference outcome for the 3 × 3 × 2 × 2 = **36 cases per implementation**, including TCP RST reply checksum and sequence-number checks for ACK and FIN+ACK. A focused vendored integration test `fragmented_ipv6_tcp_non_syn_malformed_options_match_xray_rst` checks the same flag/option/header/split matrix for Xray opt-in versus default strict parsing: Xray mode responds with a bare RST to ACK/FIN+ACK regardless of option validity; default mode drops malformed ones; inbound RST generates no response in either mode. Validation: `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed on both live TUNs, including all 36 per-implementation non-SYN cases and the pre-existing dual-stack, XHTTP/H3, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and shutdown probes. `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed (20 unit, 48 integration), and `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed (40). Vendor and workspace all-target/all-feature Clippy with `-D warnings`, Rust formatting, Shell syntax, `git diff --check` and reduced-feature `xray-tun` app build also passed (existing reduced-feature warnings remain). Stateful TCP FIN/ACK/RST semantics, other flag combinations, alternate extension header chains and more extensive RFC 7112 conformance are unverified; Xray TUN remains **Partial**.

### 13.96 Established IPv6 TCP flow with fragmented malformed ACK and FIN (2026-10-09)

The reference is still pinned Xray v26.9.9, commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`, Linux binary `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`. The previous unmatched-flow RST tests did not demonstrate how malformed options behave on an **established connection**. A new isolated network-namespace live fixture therefore opens real TCP sessions to the existing TCP echo service over the Chimera/Xray TUN path. It creates raw IPv6 SYN/ACK handshakes, verifies the SYN-ACK and the peer's initial TCP sequence number, and then sends out-of-order two-fragment `IPv6 → Fragment → [one/two padding-only Destination Options] → TCP ACK with data` and `FIN+ACK with tail data` packets. TCP checksums, flags, sequence/ACK numbers and echoed payloads are checked. For each TUN the 12-case matrix covers one or two Destination Options headers, fragment-zero carrying eight or 24 TCP bytes, and four-byte TCP option fields containing valid NOPs, invalid zero-length MSS, or invalid zero-length unknown options. Fragment IDs/source ports are isolated per flow. A **temporary nftables output RST-drop rule**, owned by the ephemeral client namespace and removed in `finally`, prevents the Linux TCP kernel from resetting raw-socket handshakes; it is only part of the test fixture, not production routing. A separate Python raw TCP receiver validates the TCP checksums and tuples.

On the measured reference and Chimera, the established flow accepts all three option layouts: the fragmented ACK data reaches the echo service and is echoed, and a subsequent fragmented FIN+ACK carries tail data that is also echoed. Receiving the reply's data and **acknowledging it before waiting for the peer FIN** matters. An exploratory run withheld the ACK for the FIN-tail echo while waiting for a peer FIN; Chimera's FIN was observed in the short window, whereas Xray's was not. This was an incomplete TCP test acknowledgment sequence, not sufficient evidence of a real shutdown incompatibility. The corrected probe ACKs received tail bytes immediately, then watches for a peer FIN and ACKs it if seen. On the final bounded live observation, Chimera returned a peer FIN in **12/12** cases, whereas pinned Xray returned a peer FIN in **4/12** cases within two seconds (every case still echoed both payloads). This is a remaining shutdown-timing observation rather than proof that Xray permanently withholds the FIN; detailed timeouts and propagation/ownership remain **unresolved**. The probe therefore asserts successful ACK/FIN-tail data echo but records peer-FIN presence separately instead of inventing timing parity. No TCP runtime modification is warranted from this evidence alone.

A focused vendored integration test `fragmented_ipv6_active_tcp_malformed_ack_and_fin_reach_stream_in_xray_mode` establishes an actual `watfaq-netstack` IPv6 TCP stream, reassembles malformed ACK data with one or two Destination Options headers, and checks that the application receives its bytes. In Xray-opt-in mode, a packet with corrupted **original** checksum does not reach the established stream, and resending valid fragments with the **same fragment ID** progresses correctly. The default non-Xray stack instead rejects malformed options; its valid retransmission at the same TCP sequence number succeeds. The subsequent FIN+ACK with a data tail reaches the stream and surfaces EOF in both modes (using valid options in default mode). The corrected full `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed the asserted **12 established-flow data/FIN-tail echo cases per TUN** and the existing IPv4/IPv6, QUIC/XHTTP/H3, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and shutdown-cleanup checks. The regression passes, as does the full vendor suite (20 unit, 49 integration), Chimera TUN library test suite (40), vendor/workspace strict all-feature Clippy, Rust formatting, shell syntax, reduced `xray-tun` app build, and `git diff --check`. This adds **no production runtime changes**. The sampled flow matrix is not full stateful TCP compatibility: retransmission timing, every option kind, connection resets, resource pressure, and alternate IPv6 extension sequences remain **Partial**.

### 13.97 Established TCP half-close timing and reverse-half usability (2026-10-09)

The fixed reference is Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`, source commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. The 13.96 live raw-TCP probe correctly verified echoed bytes from two out-of-order IPv6 fragment chains for ACK-data and FIN+ACK-tail-data, but its two-second peer-FIN observation window was too short to conclude whether Xray failed to close. A follow-up diagnostic now records **whether the peer acknowledges the input FIN** independently of **when it emits its own FIN**, with monotonic elapsed milliseconds, valid reply TCP checksums, and correct sequence/acknowledgment numbers. It continues acknowledging each echoed tail immediately. Across the same 12 cases per implementation (one/two Destination Options headers, eight/24 first-fragment TCP bytes, valid NOP/malformed MSS/malformed unknown TCP options), all input FINs were acknowledged. Chimera's peer FIN appeared immediately (sub-millisecond at this timestamp resolution). When Xray's FIN was observed, it consistently appeared around **2000–2002 ms** after sending the client's FIN+ACK, including valid-option controls; the misses in the previous two-second window are therefore a **timing-boundary artifact**, not a demonstrated malformed-option-specific failure. A longer 8-second watch of two representative option/control cases per extension configuration observed Xray's FIN around two seconds. The asserted live probe now uses **four seconds for all 12 cases** and requires: ACK-data echo, FIN-tail echo, acknowledgment of the input FIN, and an outbound peer FIN, recording FIN delay instead of demanding Chimera/Xray have identical latency. The corrected full pinned-reference namespace comparison passed: **12/12 Chimera and 12/12 Xray flows** delivered both echoes, acknowledged input FIN and returned peer FIN. Xray peer FIN latency across those 12 cases was **2000–2002 ms**; all Chimera FINs were observed at 0 ms on the probe's millisecond-resolution timer. Previously missed two-second cases therefore reflect a test deadline straddling Xray's observed ~2-second close interval.

The corresponding vendored test `fragmented_ipv6_active_tcp_malformed_ack_and_fin_reach_stream_in_xray_mode` now additionally checks that a `watfaq-netstack` application can **write outbound data after peer FIN has surfaced as read EOF** in both default and opt-in stacks. This explicitly pins TCP half-close directionality rather than treating the inbound FIN as a full-duplex close; the emitted TCP segment must carry the expected payload without RST or FIN. Existing invalid-original-checksum refusal, same-fragment-ID retry, strict-default option rejection, and FIN-tail delivery remain covered. Validation passed: the focused half-close regression; `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` (20 unit, 49 integration); `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` (40); Vendor and workspace all-target/all-feature Clippy with `-D warnings`; Rust formatting, Bash syntax and `git diff --check`; and the reduced-feature `xray-tun` app build (existing reduced-feature warnings remain). The complete live `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` passed the asserted half-close matrix and existing dual-stack TCP/UDP, QUIC/XHTTP H3, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and shutdown-cleanup checks. No production runtime code was changed. The difference between immediate Chimera FIN and roughly two-second Xray FIN remains a *measured timing difference*; absent a stated timing contract or client-visible failure, introducing a two-second delay in Chimera would weaken prompt TCP shutdown, so it is not done. Resource pressure, other peer half-close implementations, timer configurations, and long-lived TCP flows are still outside verified Xray TUN equivalence (**Partial**).

### 13.98 Established IPv6 TCP fragmented RST+ACK, duplicate suppression and same-tuple recovery (2026-10-09)

The pinned oracle is Xray `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`, `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. A new Linux namespace probe establishes a raw IPv6 TCP connection to the existing echo service, sends a valid fragmented ACK with data, confirms its echo, then retransmits the identical TCP payload with a fresh IPv6 fragment ID and checks that the peer does **not** echo duplicate application data.
