In the first pinned-Xray namespace run, **Xray accepted and echoed the no-Timestamp data in both extension-chain layouts**, without losing its established stream; the Echo TSecr remained **`0x00000008`**, reflecting the last recorded TSval rather than inventing a value for the absent option. The subsequent fresh data at **`0x00000038`** echoed with TSecr `0x00000038`. The complete TSecr sequence of server **data** replies for each Xray wrapped case was observed as **[`0xfffffff8`, `0x00000008`, `0x00000008`, `0x00000038`]**. This is an explicit source-version observation, not a requirement that all TCP stacks accept unexpected timestamp omissions. In Chimera's no-TS-negotiation case, omitting Timestamp is the **standard** path, so the same byte-stream recovery is not evidence of analogous violation handling.

As a final independent safety property, a previously delivered application segment is **replayed at its old TCP sequence** under a new IPv6 Fragment ID; neither stack delivers a duplicate Echo. Fresh subsequent in-order data must still succeed. The original 14.12 nonwrapped controls likewise remain: pinned Xray accepts a stale TSval 40001 following 41000 on a new TCP sequence and retains the previously recorded TSecr 41000; Chimera declines Timestamp, so this is plain TCP fallback. The fixed oracle's exact behavior is tracked separately from generic RFC 7323 statements. No production TCP timestamp negotiation or gVisor-style PAWS mechanism is added to smoltcp on the basis of these samples.

The vendored `established_ipv6_tcp_timestamp_decline_replay_and_recovery` regression expands from **four to eight constructor/layout/SYN-TSval cases**: default or opt-in Xray netstack × one/two IPv6 Destination Options chains × offered SYN TSval 40000 or `0xfffffff0`. It verifies the current SYN-ACK still declines Timestamp, checks byte-exact first delivery, rejects old-sequence replay even under a fresh fragment identifier, and accepts later normal TCP data. It also deliberately sends one established TCP data packet **with an unnegotiated Timestamp option** near `u32::MAX` and records whether that packet reached the application. If it did not, a standards-compliant plain-TCP retransmission of the *same TCP sequence* must restore progress; a further plain data message must succeed. This tests robustness to an anomalous peer that violates option negotiation, not Chimera's PAWS enforcement. The existing normal `tun-gateway` path and Xray-only malformed IPv6 TCP header policy are unchanged. The fixed-oracle first full namespace measurement recorded server Echo TSecr **[41000, 41000, 44000]** for the original nonwrapped Xray controls, and **[0xfffffff8, 8, 8, 0x38]** for both wrapped Xray controls. The test was strengthened to **assert the precise TSecr on each negotiated-Timestamp Echo data response**, including monotonic wrapped `0xfffffff8 → 8`, retention of `8` after the missing-Timestamp data and the later valid `0x38` update. It also **requires the fixed oracle to accept the missing-Timestamp data** in this established-flow sample; a peer not matching the pinned reference fails rather than merely producing a diagnostic log. A subsequent **source-frozen full rerun** of `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` **exited 0**, with **4/4 Chimera and 4/4 fixed-Xray** Timestamp flows, plus all prior sender/receiver SACK, malformed IPv6/ACK/RST, TCP FIN, zero-window/Window Scale, IPv4/IPv6 TCP/UDP, XHTTP/H3, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and clean shutdown probes. `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed **20 unit + 58 integration tests**, including the expanded 8-case Timestamp fallback regression; `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed **40**. Vendor and workspace all-target/all-feature strict Clippy (`-D warnings`), `cargo fmt --all -- --check`, Bash syntax, `git diff --check`, and reduced-feature `xray-tun` app build all passed (the existing **33** reduced-feature warnings remain). No production TCP changes, Git commit or worktree cleanup were made. Timestamp option disappearance under sustained load, RFC 7323 PAWS future/past window constraints, Time Wait edge behavior, TS clock skew/expiry, wrap with genuinely long delays and production-memory-pressure effects remain outside this slice. **Xray TUN stays Partial**.

### 14.14 TCP active-close, duplicate FIN and same-four-tuple SYN after close (2026-10-09)

The external oracle remains the **fixed local Xray 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)**, pinned source `ref/xray-core` at `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120` and its gVisor implementation. This iteration targets closure rather than merely stale TCP data while established. The reference gVisor `tcp/rcv.go` `handleTimeWaitSegment` ignores RST to avoid TIME_WAIT assassination, acknowledges duplicate FIN-ACK at one less than `RcvNxt` and resets its TIME_WAIT timer, and permits a higher-sequence new SYN to be redirected toward a listener. It expressly notes that this TIME_WAIT path does not implement PAWS. By contrast the vendored smoltcp 0.13.1 receiver transitions through FIN-WAIT-1/2, CLOSING, TIME-WAIT or LAST-ACK based on which endpoint sends FIN first, and the app/service's stream close forwarding determines which path is actually reachable. Thus **the fact that Xray supports a gVisor TIME_WAIT state does not mean every Xray-TUN Echo session reaches that state**.

The real isolated Linux namespace Echo target was given one **dedicated marker prefix** `chimera-timewait-active-close`: the target first echoes the exact request bytes and then **closes its TCP socket without waiting for client FIN**. All existing Echo requests and all other tests retain the previous echo-until-EOF behavior. The new `run_ipv6_tcp_timewait_reuse_probe` opens two raw IPv6 TCP connections on distinct source ports per implementation, with a valid SYN Timestamp offer and with or without two padding-only IPv6 Destination Options headers in later deliberately reverse-arriving fragmented TCP packets (only eight TCP bytes in fragment zero). It checks actual SYN/SYN-ACK Timestamp negotiation before sending subsequent options: Chimera/smoltcp declines TS, whereas the fixed Xray/gVisor agrees to it. Echo bytes, all received TCP checksums, source/destination ports, flags and sequence offsets are validated.

The primary control measures whether **the TUN-side server sends FIN before its client**. If it does, the raw client ACKs that FIN and sends its own complete out-of-order IPv6-fragmented FIN+ACK; receiving a valid final ACK of the client FIN establishes the server's **FIN-WAIT → TIME-WAIT** close direction, without introspecting private TCP socket internals. A duplicate final FIN under a **fresh fragment ID** is then replayed, observing whether a repeat ACK appears; exact duplicate-FIN ACK timing is intentionally not an equality requirement. The probe also **replays the exact previously sent application bytes under their original TCP sequence and another fresh IPv6 fragment identifier after closure**; the old TCP incarnation must not emit new application data in response. A new SYN from the **same source/destination addresses and same pair of ports**, but with client ISN substantially greater than the prior `RCV.NXT`, tests immediate four-tuple reuse. A SYN-ACK is recognized by its actual flags and ACK to the new ISN rather than any incidental ACK/RST; the raw client completes the fresh three-way handshake and verifies a genuinely new Echo. After the first live comparison established the pinned behavior, this became a **strict assertion of SYN-ACK plus new byte-exact Echo**, not only a logged observation. FIN/ACK repetition timing remains diagnostic rather than a false cross-stack equality assertion.

The initial live reference comparison exposed an important lifecycle difference: **Chimera forwarded the target's active close as a server-first FIN** (two IPv6 fragment-chain layouts, both original Echoes complete; both later same-four-tuple SYNs and fresh Echoes succeeded), but **Xray 26.9.9 forwarded the full target Echo without sending its TUN-side FIN within the fixture's original 3.2-second FIN window**. Therefore that original Xray path cannot truthfully be described as having reached server-side TIME_WAIT. The live fixture now explicitly records `active-fin` and, if the reference has not sent FIN first, **performs a legitimate client-initiated FIN/ACK shutdown and labels that path as client-first**, which corresponds to the server's CLOSE-WAIT/LAST-ACK path, not server TIME_WAIT. Both paths must finish their FIN/ACK exchange without data corruption before duplicate FIN or same-four-tuple SYN probing. Any observed attempt to reuse the old flow as new application data is an error. This is a real Xray-vs-Chimera **close propagation semantic divergence**; it is not a license to rewrite the shared TCP sender or to claim the gVisor TIME_WAIT logic itself has been externally exercised.

The new vendor regression `active_ipv6_tcp_fin_timewait_duplicate_fin_and_same_tuple_reopen` exercises **four combinations** of default netstack and opt-in Xray-TUN netstack × one/two Destination Options chains. With the real netstack TCP stream, the service performs `write_all` of a byte-exact payload followed by `shutdown()`, causing a server-first data+FIN exchange. The synthetic client ACKs the real server FIN, sends its FIN as out-of-order IPv6 fragments, requires final cumulative ACK, sends a duplicate of that FIN with a **new IPv6 Fragment ID**, then **replays a previously delivered client application payload** using its old sequence and another new fragment ID, followed by a higher-ISN SYN on **the identical four-tuple**; the accepted new stream must carry fresh data exactly once. Both constructors decline an offered Timestamp in the SYN, as already verified; this local regression tests the non-Timestamp closure path, **not** Xray/gVisor TIME_WAIT PAWS behavior. The normal `tun-gateway` path, Xray-only malformed IPv6 TCP normalizer and production TCP code are untouched. Final verification: a **source-frozen, strict-assertion** full rerun of `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` **exited 0**, with **2/2 Chimera and 2/2 pinned-Xray** active-close/client-first fallback, duplicate FIN, old application-byte replay and exact same-four-tuple new-Echo cases. Chimera showed `active-fin=True`, `final-ack=True`, `same-tuple-syn=synack`, `new-flow-echo=True` in both layouts; pinned Xray showed the same final-ACK and exact new-flow success but `active-fin=False` (client-first FIN fallback) in both layouts. Neither yielded stale Echo. The reference binary negotiated Timestamp while the vendored smoltcp socket declined it, so only the fixed oracle's closer handled negotiated Timestamp packets; the paths are deliberately **not** conflated as equivalent TIME_WAIT PAWS. All prior sender/receiver SACK, Timestamp wrap/TSecr, zero-window/Window Scale, fragmented IPv6 ACK/RST/FIN, dual-stack TCP/UDP, XHTTP/H3, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and cleanup probes also passed. The vendor crate passed **20 unit + 59 integration tests**, including the new four-combination server-first FIN/old-payload-replay/same-tuple-reuse regression; `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed **40**. Vendor and workspace all-target/all-feature strict Clippy with `-D warnings`, Rust format, Bash syntax, `git diff --check`, and reduced-feature `xray-tun` app check all passed (preexisting **33** reduced-feature warnings). No production TCP changes, Git commit or worktree cleanup were made. Unobserved gVisor TIME_WAIT cases when Xray itself actively closes TUN TCP, actual TIME_WAIT expiry (long duration), repeated FIN timer resets, TIME_WAIT RST assassination, delayed old SYN/data after accepted tuple reuse, and timestamp-enabled PAWS/TIME_WAIT under clock wrap remain out of scope; **Xray TUN stays Partial**.

### 14.15 Xray TUN server-first TIME_WAIT reachability: full-close versus TCP write-half-close (2026-10-09)

This iteration directly investigates **which target-side termination event can cause fixed Xray 26.9.9 to send TUN-side FIN before its raw TCP client**, the necessary prerequisite for observing the pinned gVisor sender in FIN-WAIT-1/FIN-WAIT-2 and then TIME_WAIT. The oracle remains **Xray 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)** from pinned `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. The prior 14.14 target terminated by fully closing its TCP socket immediately after Echo; both pinned-Xray layouts returned the data but waited for **client-first FIN** before sending their own FIN, so that evidence was necessarily a CLOSE-WAIT/LAST-ACK direction, **not** gVisor server TIME_WAIT.

**Source-boundary investigation:** `ref/xray-core/proxy/tun/stack_gvisor.go` hands each gVisor-created endpoint as a `gonet.TCPConn` to `tun.Handler.HandleConnection`, and only calls `ep.Close()` after the handler returns. The TUN handler passes the stream to `dispatcher.DispatchLink` through independent `buf.NewReader`/`buf.NewWriter` wrappers; `ref/xray-core/proxy/freedom/freedom.go` copies uplink and downlink with `task.Run` and closes the *downlink pipe* on successful target response EOF. The underlying `gonet.TCPConn` supports `CloseWrite`, but that is **not directly invoked** by the TUN handler's downlink EOF path. These code boundaries explain why the downstream target FIN need not automatically translate into TUN-side FIN, but they **do not prove** which internal task or timer prevents early FIN in every scenario; the actual wire oracle is authoritative.

To distinguish target full-close from an explicit write-half-close, the isolated TCP Echo fixture gained a second **test-only request marker**, `chimera-timewait-shutdown-write`. Upon receiving it, the destination first echoes the exact marker bytes, explicitly calls Python `socket.shutdown(SHUT_WR)` to generate downstream FIN while **keeping its read direction open**, then allows up to four seconds for a read before closing. This is different from the original `chimera-timewait-active-close` marker, which returns and closes the target socket immediately. All other Echo test requests remain unchanged. The existing raw `run_ipv6_tcp_timewait_reuse_probe` now covers **four flows per TUN**: one/two IPv6 padding Destination Options chains crossed with full target close and TCP `shutdown(SHUT_WR)`. In both layouts the test negotiates Timestamp correctly according to the actual implementation (Chimera declines; Xray enables), sends a valid fragmented Echo request with only eight TCP header bytes in fragment zero, validates the exact Echo and watches up to 3.2 seconds for **server-first FIN**. If FIN arrives first, it ACKs that FIN and sends a fragmented client FIN; if not, it records the absence and **only then** initiates a client-first FIN. Both modes require a final ACK, absence of stale Echo after old FIN/data replay under fresh IPv6 fragment IDs, and a real new three-way handshake plus byte-exact Echo on the identical four-tuple with higher client ISN.

**Observed initial live pinned-oracle comparison:** Chimera sent server-first FIN under **both full-close and shutdown-write** conditions in **both** extension layouts (**4/4**); fixed Xray returned byte-exact Echo but **did not send server-first FIN within 3.2 s for either close trigger in either layout** (**4/4**), then completed a client-first FIN/ACK exchange and accepted an independent same-four-tuple higher-ISN Echo with no stale data. This shows target-side FIN, including explicit half-close, is **insufficient on its own to exercise fixed Xray's server-side gVisor TIME_WAIT through this TUN/Freedom dispatch path**. It does **not** show that gVisor's TIME_WAIT implementation is absent, broken, immune to RST, or reachable through this particular flow; a different dispatcher termination cause or explicitly closing the TUN-side write direction would be required to test those internals as true server TIME_WAIT.

The vendored regression `active_ipv6_tcp_fin_timewait_duplicate_fin_and_same_tuple_reopen` has been expanded from four to **eight independently constructed cases**: default and Xray-TUN-compatible smoltcp netstack × one/two IPv6 Destination Options chains × server-first versus client-first FIN order. The **server-first** path drives `stream.shutdown()` after sending verified data, observes its outgoing FIN, receives an out-of-order fragmented client FIN and verifies final ACK; the **client-first** path first injects a fragmented FIN into the established stream, requires the stream read half to return EOF, then writes a response and shuts down its outgoing half, exercising CLOSE-WAIT/LAST-ACK locally. Both paths inject a duplicate FIN under a different fragment identifier, replay actual old application bytes under their old TCP sequence and a new IPv6 fragment identifier, and require a new higher-ISN SYN on the **same four-tuple** to yield independent fresh data. This confirms both close directions survive reassembly, replay and tuple reuse in the shared netstack with **no runtime behavior changes**. Final verification: the first full Linux namespace smoke observed **4/4 Chimera server-first FIN** cases and **4/4 pinned-Xray client-first FIN fallback** cases, with all eight old-FIN/old-data replay and same-four-tuple new-Echo checks succeeding. The live fixture was then strengthened to **hard-assert that FIN order on both target close triggers**, report `server-timewait-direction` explicitly, and still require final FIN ACK and true SYN-ACK plus byte-exact fresh Echo. A fresh **source-frozen** `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` full rerun **exited 0**, with **4/4 Chimera and 4/4 pinned-Xray** asserted close-style/layout combinations and all existing IPv4/IPv6 TCP/UDP, TCP SACK/DSACK/reneging, Timestamp wrap/TSecr, ACK/RST/FIN, zero-window/Window Scale, XHTTP/H3, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and shutdown cleanup probes passing. `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed **20 unit + 59 integration** tests, including the expanded eight-case FIN-order/duplicate-data/tuple-reuse regression; `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed **40**. Vendor and workspace all-target/all-feature strict Clippy (`-D warnings`), Rust format, Bash syntax and `git diff --check`, plus the reduced-feature `xray-tun` app build, all passed (its existing **33** reduced-feature warnings remain). No production code, routing policy, default `tun-gateway`, pinned oracle or existing dirty worktree was changed, and no commit or cleanup was performed. **Xray TUN stays Partial**, and actual gVisor server-side TIME_WAIT, FIN timer resets, RST assassination defense and timestamp-enabled TIME_WAIT PAWS remain unverified in the TUN/Freedom path.

### 14.16 Dispatcher/Freedom cancellation and the pinned-Xray server-FIRST-FIN boundary (2026-10-09)

The fixed oracle remains **Xray 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)** at `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`, with its locked gVisor. The 14.15 real-TUN comparison showed that downstream target `close()` and explicit `shutdown(SHUT_WR)` did not cause Xray to transmit FIN before its client within 3.2 seconds. Before claiming Xray never enters gVisor server-side TIME_WAIT, it is essential to separately exercise **session-owner cancellation**, since this is a different input from a downstream EOF.

Source inspection: `ref/xray-core/proxy/tun/stack_gvisor.go` wraps gVisor endpoints in `gonet.TCPConn` and hands them to the TUN handler; `proxy/tun/handler.go` defers `conn.Close()` until dispatch returns. `app/dispatcher/default.go` transports its two directions through pipes to `app/proxyman/outbound/handler.go`. `proxy/freedom/freedom.go` invokes `task.Run` over uplink/downlink forwarding, and `common/task/task.go` returns on context cancellation as well as task completion. Its inactivity timer is based on level policy; `features/policy/policy.go` defaults level-0 `connIdle` to **300 seconds**, with `uplinkOnly` and `downlinkOnly` each one second. `gonet.TCPConn` supports `CloseWrite()`, but normal downstream EOF follows the buffered Freedom/Dispatcher path, not a direct TUN-side `CloseWrite()` call. The session timeout can therefore trigger a different close path. A first FIN observable on the wire remains essential evidence; the existence of an internal TIME_WAIT handler or a configured short timeout alone is **not** proof that it ran.

The existing disposable Linux namespace script now runs the **complete normal Xray TUN suite first**, verifies the original Xray reference process, device and IPv4/IPv6 managed routes are removed, then starts a **second independent instance of the exact same pinned Xray binary** with the same TUN name/address/route but a **test-only level-0 policy** of `connIdle=2`, `uplinkOnly=1`, `downlinkOnly=1` seconds. The second process is confined to a specific `idle-policy` raw TCP probe. It does not alter production Chimera configuration or shorten the timeouts of the original long-stall SACK/zero-window/Timestamp/QUIC/PMTU regression suite. The second instance's TUN and both managed routes must also be removed after it exits.

The `idle-policy` probe sends real checksum-valid IPv6 TCP Echo requests on independent four-tuples, preserving one/two Destination Options chain layouts and a valid negotiated Timestamp SYN. It compares three **target-side lifecycle triggers**: immediate full socket close after Echo, explicit `shutdown(SHUT_WR)` while the target keeps reading, and a new **hold-open** marker where the target does not half-close or close at all during the initial bounded observation. The hold-open control is necessary: any server-FIRST-FIN while the target remains open cannot be attributed merely to the target's EOF. The client measures time from the request to any server-side FIN in a **5.5-second window** under the short policy, then performs a real FIN/ACK exchange, replays both the old FIN and actual old application bytes under fresh IPv6 fragment IDs, and checks a higher-ISN SYN on the **identical four-tuple** yields a new byte-exact Echo. Exact ACK timer counts are not assumed equal across smoltcp and gVisor. The original default Xray and Chimera probes remain unchanged, with hard assertions that the former is client-first and the latter server-first for their existing four trigger/layout combinations.

All results from this explicitly nondefault Xray policy are kept **separate** from the fixed Xray default-policy compatibility claim. A server-first FIN under policy cancellation would make a subsequent true gVisor TIME_WAIT test feasible, but would not retroactively prove that regular target EOF is propagated via FIN or that RST assassination/PAWS work. Conversely, an observed RST, delayed FIN or no FIN must be recorded as such rather than labeled TIME_WAIT. The remaining hard prerequisites for claiming server-side TIME_WAIT are server-FIRST-FIN, acknowledgment of that FIN, client FIN accepted/ACKed, and subsequent TIME_WAIT-specific behavior (e.g. duplicate FIN acknowledgment or RST ignored). These are distinct from successful same-four-tuple reopens following client-first closure. **Xray TUN remains Partial.**

**Fixed-oracle results.** A separate Xray 26.9.9 process with test-only level-0 connIdle=2 seconds produced **no server-first FIN within 5.5 seconds in 6/6 flows**: target full close, write-half-close, or kept fully open, each with one/two IPv6 fragment chains. All six completed a client-first FIN exchange and independently reopened the old four-tuple. This does not demonstrate that configuring short idle guarantees session cancellation or server-side TIME_WAIT in the Freedom path.

A separate test-only routing rule sends only destination TCP port **39647** to a no-response Blackhole outbound on that same second Xray process. On **2/2 valid raw IPv6 TCP flows**, Blackhole termination caused **immediate server-first FIN without RST**, the server correctly ACKed the client's FIN, and a **duplicate client FIN received a new ACK**. This is an actual on-wire match for the pinned gVisor receiver's TIME_WAIT duplicate-FIN ACK path, not just a guess from an internal state name. This demonstrates a *reachable* server-first/TIME_WAIT-style path when an outbound handler returns immediately, distinct from the ordinary Freedom downstream-EOF behavior.

The fixture also sends a **higher-ISN SYN on the same four-tuple** after duplicate FIN confirmation; under this Blackhole path the pinned oracle returned **no new SYN-ACK within 1.5 seconds, in 2/2 cases**. An initially overstrong assertion incorrectly demanded immediate tuple reuse and failed; that requirement was corrected. The final fixture **hard-asserts** server-first FIN, no RST, correct final client FIN ACK and duplicate FIN ACK, but separately reports SYN-ACK/RST/ACK/no response for attempted same-tuple reuse. This distinguishes observed TIME_WAIT-style duplicate FIN handling from unverified listener redirection or TIME_WAIT timer expiry. RST assassination and PAWS remain untested.

**Final validation:** the frozen full Linux namespace smoke script **exited 0** with all previously established default Chimera and fixed-Xray dual-stack forwarding, TCP SACK/DSACK/reneging, Timestamp, Window Scale/zero-window, IPv6 fragmentation, XHTTP/H3, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and clean shutdown tests passing, plus **6/6 short-idle Xray close-trigger** and **2/2 Blackhole FIN/duplicate-FIN** checks. Both the original Xray test instance and the test-only short-idle/Blackhole instance had their devices and managed IPv4/IPv6 routes removed. Vendor **20 unit + 59 integration** tests, Chimera TUN **40** library tests, strict Vendor/workspace Clippy, Rust formatting, Bash syntax, diff checks and the reduced-feature Xray-TUN build passed (pre-existing **33 warnings** remain). No production TCP code, default configuration, Git commit or worktree cleanup was involved. **Xray TUN remains Partial.**

### 14.17 Paired Chimera/Xray TUN TCP Blackhole close probe (2026-10-10)

The pinned oracle is unchanged: `./xray` reports **Xray 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)** with source `ref/xray-core` fixed at `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. The disposable Linux network-namespace test now gives Chimera's existing Xray TUN configuration a **TCP-only port 39647 → `immediate-blackhole`** route, separate from the pre-existing UDP QUIC SNI Blackhole rule. It invokes the same raw IPv6 TCP probe against Chimera before the regular suite and against the existing second pinned-Xray reference process afterward. Both use distinct client source ports (46220 and 46221), checksum-checked handshake/FIN traffic, client FIN acknowledgement, duplicate FIN, and a higher-ISN SYN reusing the identical four-tuple. The pinned Xray oracle retains its strict FIN/duplicate-FIN expectations; Chimera initially runs in explicitly labeled observation mode so that an interoperability difference is not silently made into an assertion of parity.

**Measured on-wire difference (two flows on each implementation):** Chimera sent server-first FIN without RST (2/2, about 202 ms after handshake ACK) and ACKed the client's FIN (2/2), but did **not** issue a new ACK to the duplicate FIN within the 0.8-second probe window (0/2); a higher-ISN SYN using the same four-tuple received SYN-ACK within 1.5 seconds (2/2). The pinned Xray reference sent immediate server-first FIN without RST (2/2), ACKed the client's FIN (2/2), issued a new duplicate-FIN ACK (2/2), and did **not** send a new SYN-ACK within the same 1.5-second window (0/2). The observed difference is compatible with a TCP close-state/lifetime divergence, **not** proof of which implementation has a particular internal TCP state or how it behaves after the measurement windows. Chimera's TCP Blackhole connector currently returns `ConnectionRefused` (`outbound.rs`), whereas the pinned Xray Blackhole handler returns normally and the TUN handler closes the underlying gVisor connection; the separate stream-owner and TCP-state effects must be traced before any production correction. Do not transplant gVisor or change the ordinary `tun-gateway` stack solely to match this probe.

**Verification:** `bash -n scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh`, `git diff --check`, then `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` **exit 0**. The full suite's established dual-stack forwarding, VLESS/XHTTP H3 interop, QUIC route-only, TCP SACK/FIN/RST, IPv6 fragment/ICMP/PMTU, both Xray idle-policy/Blackhole oracle cases, route rollback and TUN cleanup remain passing; the reduced-feature build still emitted its previously recorded 33 warnings. This iteration changes only the test fixture and compatibility/design documentation, not production TCP handling. **Xray TUN remains Partial**; duplicate-FIN handling, immediate four-tuple reuse, longer TIME_WAIT deadlines and PAWS are not declared compatible.

### 14.18 Locate TCP TIME-WAIT ownership behind the Blackhole gap (2026-10-10)

**Scope:** characterization tests only; no production TCP, outbound, dispatcher or TUN code is changed. The existing Xray 26.9.9 reference result in section 14.17 remains the external compatibility contract. Trace on the Chimera side: `connect_tcp_via_outbound` in `outbound.rs` returns `ConnectionRefused` for Blackhole; `session/dispatcher.rs::process_stream_with_context` awaits inbound `server_stream.shutdown()` on failed outbound selection and then returns; `tun_gateway.rs::run_server` owns the spawned per-flow task and wraps the accepted `TcpStream` in `GatewayTcpStream`. `vendor/watfaq-netstack/src/tcp_stream.rs` translates shutdown into the `write_shutdown` flag and stream Drop into `socket_dropped`; `tcp_listener.rs::poll_sockets` drains pending sends, calls `socket.close()` and retains only sockets whose `socket.is_active()` is true. The pinned dependency `smoltcp 0.13.1` explicitly reports `is_active() == false` for `State::TimeWait` (its `src/socket/tcp.rs`), so the listener's retention policy removes sockets on that transition rather than preserving the state for later duplicate FIN handling. Separately, `poll_packets` creates a new listening socket on a fresh SYN; safe protection against immediate four-tuple reuse requires considering that admission path, not just the retention predicate.

**Isolated reproduction:** new `vendor/watfaq-netstack/tests/integration_test.rs::tcp_blackhole_like_stream_close_obeys_xray_timewait_opt_in` uses in-memory IPv6 TCP packets, a Blackhole destination port 39647, and all four combinations of default/Xray-opt-in constructors × direct `Drop`/explicit `AsyncWrite::shutdown` then `Drop`. Each combination completes SYN/SYN-ACK, observes server-first FIN without RST, verifies ACK of client FIN, then observes **no fresh duplicate-FIN ACK within 120 ms** and **a new same-four-tuple SYN-ACK within 800 ms**. Those are intentionally bounded local behavior-characterization assertions, not successful Xray parity tests and not a substitute for the longer 0.8-second/1.5-second Linux namespace oracle. Both shutdown modes show the same result, reducing the likelihood that dispatcher choice of `shutdown` versus `Drop` alone explains section 14.17's gap. Do not infer TIME_WAIT expiration or gVisor's PAWS/timeout behavior from these bounds.

**Verification:** exact test `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml --test integration_test tcp_blackhole_like_stream_close_obeys_xray_timewait_opt_in -- --exact --nocapture` passed (1 test; four subcases). Full vendor test suite passed **20 unit + 60 integration**; strict vendor all-target/all-feature Clippy passed; `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed **40**; vendor and workspace formatting checks passed. The full live Xray 26.9.9 namespace comparison from 14.17 remains the most recent physical-device oracle; it was not repeated after this test-only addition. **Next migration slice** must design bounded Xray-opt-in TIME_WAIT retention with explicit source-tuple SYN admission, resource limits, timer cleanup and shutdown ownership, with negative malformed/replay probes and the same pinned Xray real-TUN case before any compatibility claim. The ordinary Chimera-only `tun-gateway` behavior must remain unchanged.

### 14.19 Bounded Xray-TUN-only smoltcp TIME-WAIT retention (2026-10-10)

**Implemented scope and ownership.** The prior section 14.18 recorded the gap before this change; its probe is now a post-fix opt-in versus default-mode regression. The canonical pinned reference stays `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120` and local binary **Xray 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)**. The fix is confined to `vendor/watfaq-netstack/src/tcp_listener.rs` and the existing explicit `new_with_mtu_xray_tun_compat` path, not the shared default `tun-gateway`, outbound protocol or dispatcher. smoltcp 0.13.1 returns `is_active() == false` for `State::TimeWait` even though its socket can still challenge-ACK old packets. The Xray-opt-in socket-state loop now retains exactly that state until its built-in **10-second close timer** finishes. A shared, listener-owned set of source/destination socket-address tuples prevents the packet-admission loop from instantiating a second smoltcp TCP listener for a SYN on a retained TIME-WAIT tuple; such a SYN is still passed to the existing TCP socket. The reservation disappears as the socket becomes inactive after TIME-WAIT, and is cleared if the TCP socket set is rebuilt after an internal poll panic. Normal listener shutdown owns/drops both the socket set and reservation set. Because retained sockets keep their `TcpStreamHandle` alive, the existing **512-stream limit** also bounds the retained TIME-WAIT state; increased occupancy is an intentional Xray-opt-in tradeoff, not a general throughput improvement.

**Local regression:** `tcp_blackhole_like_stream_close_obeys_xray_timewait_opt_in` now validates default/Xray-opt-in constructors × Drop/shutdown-then-Drop. Xray mode acknowledges duplicate FIN and rejects a premature higher-ISN same-four-tuple SYN-ACK, while the default mode retains immediate tuple reuse; an Xray-opt-in case waits beyond smoltcp's 10-second close timeout and confirms the SYN-ACK is admitted again. The existing eight-case fragmented IPv6 FIN-direction test now distinguishes Xray-opt-in server-first TIME-WAIT (duplicate FIN ACK and no immediate tuple reuse) from the default mode and client-first LAST-ACK/CLOSED cases. The fixed namespace fixture retains the strict pinned Xray Blackhole oracle and now additionally requires matching Chimera duplicate FIN ACK and no immediate same-tuple SYN-ACK. The normal Chimera Freedom server-first FIN tests similarly require a duplicate FIN ACK and no premature same-four-tuple SYN-ACK, without rewriting the pinned reference's client-first Freedom expectations.

**On-wire verification:** `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` **exited 0** with **2/2 Chimera and 2/2 pinned Xray TCP/39647 Blackhole** flows showing server-first FIN, no RST, final client FIN ACK, a fresh duplicate-FIN ACK and no new SYN-ACK in the 1.5-second reuse window. Chimera's **4/4 Freedom server-first** close-style/IPv6-chain cases also acknowledged duplicate FIN without old application data or premature tuple reacceptance. All previously established dual-stack TCP/UDP, SACK/retransmit, IPv6 fragment, XHTTP H3, QUIC, ICMP/PMTU, original and test-only pinned Xray, route rollback and TUN shutdown cleanup checks passed. The separately run vendored suite passed **20 unit + 60 integration** tests, its all-target/all-feature Clippy `-D warnings` passed, and `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed **40**. Rustfmt, Bash syntax and diff whitespace checks passed. The exceptional socket-set-panic reservation clear was added after the full namespace executable was built and was subsequently checked with the focused regression and strict Clippy; the new branch itself was not fault-injected in the namespace run. The reduced-feature Xray-TUN build still emitted its previously documented **33 warnings**.

**Boundaries remaining:** Xray's normal Freedom/Dispatcher path still defers server-first FIN relative to Chimera; the Blackhole FIN timing is likewise not byte-for-byte equal (Chimera ~202 ms after ACK in this run; pinned Xray immediate). The 10-second smoltcp TIME-WAIT timer is not evidence of parity with gVisor's duration or expiration policy. RST assassination, PAWS/Timestamp, aggressive TIME-WAIT churn and memory/CPU impact at the 512-stream ceiling remain unverified. Do not mark Xray TUN Fully Compatible. The next slice should compare RST/PAWS/expiration and long-lived resource pressure against the pinned oracle; keep default `tun-gateway` isolated and preserve all existing dirty worktree state.

### 14.20 Xray TUN TIME_WAIT RST-assassination and bounded short-flow churn (2026-10-10)

**Baseline and observed difference.** Retain Xray 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64) and pinned `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. The same namespace Blackhole TCP/39647 fixture now adds two diagnostic cases after the original two strict FIN cases. Case 2 retransmits the client FIN with an earlier TCP TSval; pinned Xray negotiates a Timestamp option in its SYN-ACK and **acknowledged the old-TS FIN** (1/1), while Chimera/smoltcp did not negotiate TCP Timestamps and emitted **no new ACK within 0.5 seconds** (1/1). This difference is not evidence that either stack correctly implements or violates PAWS; the two connections did not have equivalent Timestamp negotiation. Case 3 injects a **checksum-valid fragmented IPv6 RST+ACK** at the next client receive sequence after the final FIN exchange, preserving the nftables shield against unrelated host-generated RST packets. On the original Xray-TUN-only TIME_WAIT retention implementation, Chimera admitted a same-four-tuple higher-ISN SYN (1/1). Pinned Xray returned **no SYN-ACK in the same 1.5-second window** (1/1). Its pinned gVisor dependency `gvisor.dev/gvisor@v0.0.0-20260122175437-89a5d21be8f0/pkg/tcpip/transport/tcp/rcv.go` explicitly drops RST packets in TIME_WAIT (RFC 1337), avoiding premature TIME_WAIT assassination.

**Bounded implementation and ownership.** Only the Xray-TUN-opt-in vendored TCP packet path changes: after validating the original TCP checksum, it silently discards RST traffic whose source/destination tuple is presently reserved by an actual smoltcp TIME_WAIT socket. Ordinary `tun-gateway` and all established-connection RST handling are unchanged. The same tuple reservation continues to be created by the socket-state owner, removed at smoltcp's 10-second TIME_WAIT expiry, cleared on exceptional socket-set replacement, and dropped with the listener at shutdown. The test fixture treats *all four* Blackhole flows (including the post-close RST case) as strictly requiring no early same-tuple SYN-ACK; the stale-TS result remains diagnostic because Chimera does not negotiate the Timestamp option.

**Resource and regression evidence.** The existing `tcp_blackhole_like_stream_close_obeys_xray_timewait_opt_in` memory-TUN test now includes a checksum-valid in-window RST before checking immediate tuple reacceptance in its Xray/shutdown subcase. The new `xray_timewait_burst_preserves_new_admission_and_recovers_on_expiry` integration test closes **32 different IPv6 TCP four-tuples** to TIME_WAIT in the opt-in stack, requires those old tuples remain protected while a separate new tuple can establish, then waits past the 10-second smoltcp close timer and verifies an old tuple can be reopened. It also verifies listener shutdown, but is not a sustained-load, full 512-stream or physical-network capacity test. **Final post-fix checks:** `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed **20 unit + 61 integration** tests; `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings` passed; `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed **40** tests, and Rustfmt, Bash syntax and diff whitespace checks passed. The final `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` **exited 0**: the two original Blackhole FIN oracles and the two new stale-Timestamp/postclose-RST probes ran against both Chimera and pinned Xray; all **4/4 per implementation** showed correct server-first FIN/no RST, final client FIN ACK, duplicate FIN ACK and no new SYN-ACK within the bounded reuse window, including the RST case. The stale-TS FIN reply remains a difference, as documented above. Original TCP/UDP, XHTTP/H3, SACK/retransmission, QUIC, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and TUN teardown tests remained passing. **Xray TUN remains Partial**: longer TIME_WAIT retention, comprehensive RFC 1337 edge cases, PAWS/Timestamp negotiation and CPU/memory behavior at the full cap are not yet established.

### 14.21 TCP Timestamp capability review and actual 512-handle admission boundary (2026-10-10)

**Timestamp/PAWS boundary:** fixed Xray 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64), pinned `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`, negotiates TCP Timestamps on its TUN Blackhole SYN-ACK; Chimera currently declines them. Inspection of `smoltcp 0.13.1/src/socket/tcp.rs` found an opt-in `set_tsval_generator(Some(fn() -> u32))` API, but the receive path updates and echoes `last_remote_tsval` without a clear stale timestamp comparison implementing PAWS. Thus *simply enabling the generator* would change a public protocol capability without validated stale-TS rejection, wraparound and missing-TS handling. No generator or production TCP packet behavior was changed in this iteration. `established_ipv6_tcp_timestamp_decline_replay_and_recovery` passed and continues to enforce the actual current no-Timestamp-negotiation contract even when offered in an IPv6 SYN. PAWS, longer gVisor TIME_WAIT expiry and precise Blackhole FIN timing remain open.

**Real listener capacity test:** new ignored-by-default `vendor/watfaq-netstack/tests/integration_test.rs::xray_tcp_512_active_socket_cap_and_rst_recovery` drives the actual opt-in TCP listener (not just a mock resource-count helper), holding **512 simultaneous SYN-RECEIVED socket handles** and consuming the corresponding accepted `TcpStream` objects. It verifies that the **513th SYN neither produces a SYN-ACK nor an additional accepted stream**; a checksum-valid RST for an existing non-TIME_WAIT flow plus dropping that stream frees a slot for a different SYN, and the listener then shuts down without stalling. Explicit command:

```sh
cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml --test integration_test xray_tcp_512_active_socket_cap_and_rst_recovery -- --exact --ignored --nocapture
```

The explicit test passed on the development Linux host; a repeated run accepted 512 SYNs in approximately **142 ms** and observed approximately **16 MiB VmRSS**. Those figures are *fixture observations only*, not throughput or maximum RSS specifications. The large TCP buffers were not filled, and the flow state is SYN-RECEIVED rather than 512 concurrent TIME_WAIT connections. The earlier 32-flow TIME_WAIT test is complementary: it tests completed FIN state and expiry but does not approach the 512 cap. The new test stays ignored by default because large socket-count allocation is not appropriate for ordinary low-resource CI runs. The next independent slice should measure memory while buffers are populated and send/receive traffic is sustained, followed by Xray-specific timestamp negotiation plus a proven PAWS state machine. **Xray TUN remains Partial.**

### 14.22 Xray-only TCP Timestamp negotiation against pinned Xray 26.9.9 (2026-10-10)

**Revision to the 14.21 boundary.** The fixed Xray oracle is `./xray` version 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64), reference commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`. Source inspection of its pinned `gvisor.dev/gvisor@v0.0.0-20260122175437-89a5d21be8f0/pkg/tcpip/transport/tcp/rcv.go::handleTimeWaitSegment` says **"As we do not yet support PAWS"** and ignores RST during TIME_WAIT. This corrects a prior conservative assumption that Xray Timestamp negotiation implied complete PAWS protection. Its observed established-TCP oracle accepts fresh-sequence data carrying an older non-wrapped TSval, 32-bit wrapped TSvals, and data lacking a Timestamp after negotiation; ordinary old-sequence replay does not reach the application a second time. These are bounded pinned-version observations, *not* a general implementation of RFC 7323 PAWS.

**Opt-in implementation.** Only `vendor/watfaq-netstack/src/tcp_listener.rs` when the explicit Xray-TUN constructor is used: configure smoltcp 0.13.1 `Socket::set_tsval_generator(Some(...))` immediately before `listen()`, using monotonic millisecond elapsed time plus a randomized per-process 32-bit offset (wrapping at 32 bits). The existing default `tun-gateway` retains its original no-Timestamp contract. When the peer SYN lacks a Timestamp option, smoltcp declines negotiation even in Xray mode. This is a limited negotiated-wire feature; smoltcp's API does not offer per-connection timestamp offsets matching gVisor.

**Verification:** `xray_tcp_timestamp_negotiates_only_if_syn_offers_option` covers both constructors × SYN with/without Timestamp and checks the SYN-ACK TSecr; `established_ipv6_tcp_timestamp_negotiation_replay_and_recovery` covers default/opt-in, one/two IPv6 Destination Options, normal and wrapped offered timestamps, old-sequence replay and continuing byte-exact application delivery. The existing FIN/TIME_WAIT direction test now asserts the SYN-ACK Timestamp only for the Xray mode. The final vendored regression passed **20 unit + 62 integration** tests, with the separate 512-stream capacity test intentionally ignored by default and explicitly run to PASS (512 accepted, 513th denied, capacity recovery); strict workspace Clippy, format and the Chimera TUN library's **40 tests** passed. The fixed-Xray Linux namespace test covers four Timestamp cases per implementation (normal/wrapped × one/two fragmented options chains), Blackhole cases and previously verified dual-stack routes, TCP, QUIC and H3. **Final real-TUN run:** `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` with the finalized randomized-Timestamp-offset binary **exited 0**, including all eight Timestamp reports (four cases per implementation), all eight Blackhole/TIME_WAIT cases, dual-stack TCP/UDP, QUIC and VLESS/XHTTP H3, ICMP/PMTU, IPv6 fragmentation, RST, route-conflict rollback and TUN shutdown cleanup. Its strictly observed TSecr recency difference remains open.

**Remaining on-wire difference:** In a non-wrapped fresh-sequence segment with **older TSval**, Chimera/smoltcp generates TSecr corresponding to that old accepted value, whereas the pinned Xray/gVisor receiver retains the previous recent TSval and echoes it instead. Both deliver the exact new application data. This distinct TSecr behavior and generator offset scheme are still unaligned. Do not claim complete PAWS, TCP option or TUN compatibility; track this as **Xray TUN Partial** until a separately validated, state-owned recency implementation can reproduce gVisor semantics without changing default TUN or the integrity of previously verified flows.

### 14.23 Xray-only TCP TSecr recent timestamp normalization (2026-10-10)

**Pinned oracle and source rule.** Reuse Xray 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64), source `ref/xray-core` at `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`, and the pinned `gvisor.dev/gvisor@v0.0.0-20260122175437-89a5d21be8f0/pkg/tcpip/transport/tcp/endpoint.go::updateRecentTimestamp` rule: update the recent timestamp only when its wrap-aware signed sequence value is **newer** and the segment SEQ is no later than the highest transmitted cumulative ACK. The fixed Xray oracle accepts some fresh-sequence data with older TSval but **does not replace** the TSecr recent value. Prior Chimera TSecr observations `[41000,40001,44000]` differed from pinned Xray `[41000,41000,44000]`.

**Implementation and ownership.** A deliberately bounded adaptation in `vendor/watfaq-netstack/src/tcp_listener.rs` runs **only** inside the explicit `xray-tun` TCP input pump. On admission of a SYN that offers Timestamp it registers a small per-four-tuple state holding the SYN TSval, a conservative in-order receive sequence cursor and a **Weak** reference to the actual `TcpStreamHandle`; non-Timestamp/default TUN flows have no state or wire rewrite. For checksum-verified incoming TCP packets, compare the new TSval using signed 32-bit modular ordering; when stale or from a future sequence, preserve the cached recent TSval by rewriting only the TSval forwarded internally to smoltcp, then **recompute the TCP checksum**. This retains smoltcp's existing data, FIN/RST and sequence decisions and avoids silently dropping old-timestamp traffic that pinned Xray accepts. Pending state is bounded by the 512-stream cap: expired weak owners are pruned before a new insert at capacity and on an old tuple's next packet; a new SYN replaces the previous incarnation, and all state dies with the packet task on listener shutdown. This is a consciously narrow approximation to gVisor's `maxSentAck` gate: the local cursor tracks *in-order observed TCP data*, not the smoltcp sender's exact cumulative ACK. Arbitrary out-of-order, zero-window, spoofed high-TSval and asymmetric loss cases still require explicit reference-driven testing; do **not** call this a full PAWS or TSecr policy implementation.

**Focused evidence:** `xray_tsecr_recency_retains_newest_value_through_old_tsval_and_wrap` and `xray_tsval_field_rejects_truncated_options` cover signed wrap comparison, stale/missing TS and TCP option framing. `xray_tcp_timestamp_tsecr_recent_is_not_regressed_by_older_data` checks **real in-memory TCP input → read delivery → smoltcp ACK TSecr** for ordinary old TSval and valid 32-bit wrap, not merely the helper. The Linux namespace `run_ipv6_tcp_timestamp_replay_probe` now enforces TSecr recency for **both** Chimera and pinned Xray in all four IPv6 option-chain × wrapped/unwrapped cases. Chimera's observed non-wrapped result became `[41000,41000,44000]` and wrapped result remained `[4294967288,8,8,56]`, identical to the pinned Xray results under these tests. Existing FIN/TIME_WAIT, Blackhole, RST and duplicate-data probes are preserved. **Final verification:** `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed **22 unit + 63 integration** (one explicit 512-capacity test ignored by default, then run separately and passed); `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings` and `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` (**40 passed**) succeeded. The finalized source after the new-SYN/no-Timestamp state cleanup passed `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` (**exit 0**, eight strict Timestamp reports, eight Blackhole/TIME_WAIT cases and unchanged dual-stack forwarding, VLESS/XHTTP H3, QUIC, ICMP/PMTU, routing rollback and TUN shutdown cleanup). The strict check compares TSecr on both implementations, and observed non-wrapped/wrapped values now match in the bounded fixtures. **Xray TUN stays Partial**: PAWS rejection, exact per-connection TS offset, actual gVisor cumulative-ACK cursor and untested reordering remain outstanding.

### 14.24 Bounded TSecr gap-repair and future-ACK cursor characterization (2026-10-10)

**Scope and pinned reference.** Continue with `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120` and Xray **26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)**, which uses the pinned gVisor `Endpoint::updateRecentTimestamp`: a timestamp advances only if newer in signed 32-bit modular order and segment SEQ is not beyond `MaxSentAck`. The preceding Xray-only recency helper counted only segments whose start equaled its `next_seq`. A failing unit regression demonstrated that after accepting an out-of-order tail `[1011,1021)` before the missing head `[1001,1011)`, the helper incorrectly stopped its cursor at 1011 rather than the new contiguous frontier 1021. Subsequent valid `SEQ=1021` with newer TSval was therefore incorrectly prevented from advancing TSecr despite the TCP stream's gap being repaired.

**Minimal Xray-only implementation.** The existing `XrayTimestampRecent` socket-owned, weak-owner, checksum-checked packet-pump metadata now also stores up to **32** future TCP sequence ranges `[start,end)` within **256 KiB** of the current contiguous cursor; it does not store any payload. A leading/gap-repair or overlapping segment advances the contiguous frontier and absorbs all adjacent previously seen ranges, with modular signed sequence comparison across 32-bit wrap. Segments ahead of that frontier may be buffered as metadata but **cannot prematurely update TSecr**; zero-length control traffic never advances the byte cursor, and far-future/out-of-window sequence numbers are not cached. The complete object is still confined to explicitly enabled Xray TUN Timestamp connections, with the existing 512-socket admission limit and old socket-owner cleanup. Default `tun-gateway` receives no new option, timestamp tracking or sequence normalization.

**Focused evidence.** A new `xray_tsecr_gap_reassembly_advances_ack_cursor_without_future_ts_poisoning` test began by reproducing the old 1011-versus-1021 failure and now passes, including wrapped TCP sequence numbers and post-gap newer TSval. A companion `xray_tsecr_future_ack_and_zero_length_window_probe_do_not_advance_cursor` checks that future-sequence zero-payload ACKs do not poison recency and that zero-length keepalive/window-probe metadata cannot advance the receive-byte cursor. These are **state-model tests**, not proof of parity for actual smoltcp zero-window capacity or gVisor abnormal-ACK responses. The new in-memory `xray_tcp_tsecr_out_of_order_gap_repair_matches_ack_frontier` sends an out-of-order payload tail, verifies no early application delivery, fills the gap, verifies byte-exact concatenation and TSecr=41000, then verifies a new contiguous message produces TSecr=42000. The pinned live Linux namespace probe now appends this operation to one existing Timestamp flow on **both** Chimera and Xray (other three normal/wrapped × IPv6 extension-chain Timestamp cases remain unchanged). The new probe requires the buffered tail not be prematurely echoed and enforces TSecr=45000 on the gap repair and TSecr=46000 on subsequent fresh data. Both implementations produced the same values in the on-wire run.

**Limitations.** The 32-range bounded metadata is an approximation to gVisor's *actually transmitted cumulative ACK*, not a subscriber to smoltcp's ACK-emission state. Out-of-window reception, full/zero receive windows, >32 disjoint holes, forged high ACKs, prolonged loss, PAWS enforcement, exact FIN timing and memory/performance stress therefore remain unproven; no production ACK/window rewrite was attempted. Keep **Xray TUN Partial**, do not broaden the compatibility matrix to these untested cases.

### 14.25 TCP Timestamp abnormal-ACK and advertised zero-window oracle (2026-10-10)

**Scope and result.** For the **pinned** Xray 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64), `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120`, the current gVisor TCP receiver first checks whether the *incoming sequence* is acceptable; its sender `handleRcvdSegment` updates recent Timestamp before subsequently evaluating the ACK number range. A future-sequence ACK must not poison recent TSecr, but a packet with acceptable SEQ and a numerically impossible **future ACK number** can still update TSecr. This was verified by real IPv6 TUN observation, not assumed from RFC guidance.

**Bounded on-wire reference test.** The existing timestamp/replay probe now extends the non-wrapped single-Destination-Options-chain flow, after the previously verified `TSecr=45000 → 46000` gap-repair checks. It sends (1) a pure ACK with far-forward client SEQ and TSval 61000, followed by fresh data at TSval 46010; (2) a pure ACK at the valid client SEQ but ACK number +0x200000 beyond the server's sent range and TSval 62000, followed by valid data TSval 46020; (3) an ACK advertising **client receive window zero**, then a reopening ACK and fresh data TSval 46040. The checked, checksum-valid fresh application echoes on **both Chimera and pinned Xray** carried TSecr **46010, 62000, 62000** respectively, without reset or data corruption. The fixture now requires this same strict tuple for each implementation. The zero-window control constrains the **client's advertised receive window (Chimera's transmit side)**; it does **not** test filling the Chimera/gVisor *inbound receive buffer* to window zero. Likewise, this probe records TSecr after the next valid application data, not whether the anomalous control packet itself triggered a challenge ACK.

**In-memory regression and decision.** `xray_tcp_timestamp_tsecr_recent_is_not_regressed_by_older_data` also sends the far-forward SEQ and far-future ACK cases through the actual Xray-opt-in in-memory TCP stack, verifying subsequent byte-exact application delivery and ACK TSecr **42010 and 62000**. The existing local ordinary-TUN isolation test remains unchanged. **No production timestamp logic is changed in this slice:** filtering all impossible ACK numbers before refreshing recent TSecr would *reduce* compatibility with this fixed reference. Remaining work includes a truly zero **server receive window**, full receive-buffer pressure, ACK emission cursor observation, additional invalid-window/overlap shapes, and resource-load behavior. `xray-tun` remains **Partial**; do not infer complete PAWS or TSecr policy parity.

**Final validation and teardown fixture incident.** With the new strict anomaly-TSecr assertions, `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` finally **exited 0** after all 8 Timestamp reports (4 per implementation), 8 Blackhole/TIME_WAIT cases, standard TCP/UDP dual-stack, XHTTP/H3 and QUIC, ICMP/PMTU, route-conflict rollback and teardown checks. `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed **24 unit + 64 integration** (one 512-connection stress test ignored by default), `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings`, `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` (**40 passed**), Rustfmt, Bash syntax and diff whitespace passed. The **first** full namespace run produced identical, passing protocol results but exited **1** in its very last IPv6 system-route cleanup check after printing the success banner. That was not disregarded: the fixture now permits up to **2 seconds** for kernel netlink interface/route disappearance after waiting for the process to exit, while leaving all four device/route leak assertions active and printing the exact IPv6 route and device status on persistent failure. The final full run passed those strict checks. This is consistent with transient teardown visibility but does **not prove its root cause** or that every route-teardown race is eliminated; retain the first failure log for regression analysis.



**Final verification.** `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed **24 unit + 64 integration** tests (one prior 512-socket capacity test ignored by default); `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings` and `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` (**40/40**) passed. Workspace and standalone vendor Rustfmt, Bash syntax, and `git diff --check` passed. `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` exited **0**: eight Timestamp cases (four per endpoint, with one new strict out-of-order frontier case per endpoint), eight Blackhole cases, and pre-existing dual-stack TCP/UDP, IPv6 fragmentation, VLESS/XHTTP H3, QUIC, ICMP/PMTU, TCP SACK/zero-window, route rollback and teardown all passed. The extra zero-length/future-ACK unit test was added during the live run and then included in the final 24-unit rerun; it does not modify runtime behavior. No protocol scope beyond the bounded tests is promoted to complete.

### 14.26 Actual server receive-zero-window Timestamp recency (2026-10-11)

**Reproduction.** Unlike §14.25's *client-advertised* zero window, the new explicit `xray_tcp_actual_receive_zero_window_timestamp_probe` pauses application reads on an accepted Xray-TUN TCP stream. smoltcp and the application stream each have a 256 KiB receive buffer. After **524,280 bytes** of checksum-valid data, the outgoing server ACK advertised **TCP receive window 0** (the SYN offered Window Scale and Timestamp). A 1-byte probe at the next client SEQ with `TSval=75000` was not acknowledged. After the application consumed 32 KiB, the test waited for an actual **nonzero server-window update** before retransmitting that sequence byte with `TSval=50000`. The prior implementation returned **TSecr=75000**, proving an unaccepted zero-window payload poisoned the Xray-specific recency adapter. No extra bytes were accepted from the first probe.

**Xray-only correction.** `vendor/watfaq-netstack/src/tcp_listener.rs` now mirrors the socket owner's *advertised receive-window-zero* condition via a release/acquire atomic stored on the already-owned `TcpStreamHandle`. For nonempty TCP data or FIN arriving while that condition holds, only the Xray-opt-in Timestamp packet path preserves the prior recent TSval and does not move its mirrored receive-sequence cursor. The segment still reaches smoltcp for normal acceptability/window handling; only the internal Timestamp value is normalized after the **original TCP checksum check**, and its checksum is recomputed. Zero-length ACKs remain eligible to update recent TSval, preserving the pinned-Xray behavior observed in §14.25. Crucially, smoltcp 0.13.1 advertises a scaled receive window: its 256 KiB ring has **shift 3**, so fewer than **8 free bytes** already advertises zero; without Window Scale the quantum is **1 byte**. The shift depends on whether the peer offered Window Scale in the SYN, and a unit test pins both cases. Default `tun-gateway` does not act on this state.

**Measured recovery and boundaries.** The fixed ignored-by-default in-memory probe observes true advertised window zero, rejects the high-Timestamp 1-byte probe, confirms the window reopening ACK, and receives **TSecr=50000** after the accepted retransmission. It drains the complete original data and verifies the rejected probe was not delivered. Run explicitly: `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml --test integration_test xray_tcp_actual_receive_zero_window_timestamp_probe -- --exact --ignored --nocapture`. The pinned Xray 26.9.9/gVisor `receiver.acceptable` sequence/window check before timestamp updating supports this behavior, but **a separately stalled Xray application-side *server receive-zero-window* packet oracle is not yet in the live namespace suite**. The existing pinned real-TUN suite still tests client-advertised zero window and broader dual-stack interoperability. Near-zero window races, other window scales, exact gVisor output ACK timing, PAWS and sustained full-load equivalence remain open. **Final checks:** `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed **25 unit + 64 integration tests**, with **two** resource-pressure tests ignored by default (the new server-zero-window test was run separately and passed **three consecutive times**). `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed **40** tests; `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings`, both Rustfmt checks, Bash syntax and Git diff whitespace checks passed. The final unchanged general interoperability fixture `XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` **exited 0** with eight Timestamp and eight Blackhole reports and all pre-existing IPv4/IPv6 forwarding, XHTTP/H3, QUIC, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and TUN teardown checks. This does **not** add a pinned Xray *server-side* zero receive-window parity claim. **Xray TUN stays Partial.**

### 14.27 Pinned Xray 26.9.9 *server-side* TCP zero-receive-window oracle (2026-10-11)

**Scope and isolation.** Continue using `ref/xray-core` at `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120` and `./xray` version `26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)`. A new **explicit opt-in** test in `scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` uses real Linux IPv6 TUN packets toward the independent dual-stack echo namespace, but targets TCP **39648**, whose application deliberately stops reading until an out-of-band release-file signal. This tests the **inbound/server receive window**, unlike the existing client-advertised zero-window/persist test on port 39643. The sender uses original-checksum-valid TCP with Timestamp and client WS=3, parses each server SYN-ACK Window Scale (Chimera advertises **3**, pinned Xray advertises **8**), and uses the *actual* scaled server ACK window for bounded, backpressured packets. It requires advertised **zero**, a rejected next-sequence one-byte payload carrying `TSval=75000`, target-release and server nonzero-window update, then ACK of a same-sequence `TSval=50000` retransmission with **TSecr=50000**. Each implementation has its own four-tuple/target; differences in accepted buffer size are not treated as protocol failures.

**Reference finding.** In the successful sixth paired diagnostic, Chimera briefly advertised zero at **671,736 bytes** but then issued a nonzero window update while its downstream forwarding buffers were still ramping up; the new fixture detected this *transient* zero and resumed filling instead of declaring a stable block. Chimera's later stable zero was at **1,196,016 bytes**, WS=3, and its successful recovery ACK had TSecr=50000. Fixed Xray likewise briefly reopened around **656,384 bytes** (`window=2048`), then reached stable zero at **1,048,320 bytes**, WS=8. Its zero-window probe was not ACKed, target-read release reopened the advertised server window (`window=2048`), and the accepted retransmission ACK echoed **TSecr=50000**. Both therefore matched in this bounded, *real pinned-binary* server receive-backpressure scenario, despite different window scaling/capacity. This closes the exact missing pinned-binary evidence noted in §14.26, **not** general flow-control or PAWS parity.

**Fixture stabilization and honest negative records.** Earlier experimental iterations encountered a mis-escaped SYN's binary TCP options, an overly short **15-second** target receive-idle timeout (which could cause RST after release), and a brief initial zero advertisement incorrectly treated as persistent backpressure. These are distinct fixture confounders; no single one has been proved to explain every early recovery timeout. The target now records acceptance, release and bytes drained; it keeps the TCP connection open beyond the measurement window. The raw sender waits briefly for asynchronously advertised *nonzero* window updates after each candidate zero; if found, it continues filling. The diagnostic sends stale-TS pure ACKs at bounded intervals during reopen observation; it allows bounded retransmission of the accepted-data probe. The test explicitly fails on missing zero window, unexpected acceptance of the high-TS data, missing reopen or incorrect TSecr, but **both** implementations are always exercised even if one fails. A failure counter is reported after the unchanged regular forwarding and teardown checks. To avoid making an expensive and previously fixture-sensitive pressure experiment a routine CI requirement, the full independent oracle is enabled only via:

```sh
XRAY_TUN_SERVER_RX_PRESSURE=1 XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh
```

The normal `XRAY_BIN=./xray` namespace smoke continues without this optional stress case. The fixture caps attempted data at **20 MiB** and pressure duration at **100 seconds**, in addition to bounded window reopen/retransmit timeouts. No new production Rust, default `tun-gateway`, routing policy or reference source is changed. **Final pinned-binary run:** `XRAY_TUN_SERVER_RX_PRESSURE=1 XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` **exited 0**. It passed both real server-window probes, eight Timestamp and eight Blackhole/TIME_WAIT reports, previously covered dual-stack forwarding, H3/QUIC, ICMP/PMTU, route conflict rollback and strict device/route teardown. The unchanged local Rust suite passed **25 unit + 64 integration** vendored tests (two ignored by default) and **40 Chimera TUN library** tests; strict workspace Clippy, Rustfmt, Bash syntax and diff checks passed. **Remaining Partial:** server window changes under real loss/reordering or simultaneous competing flows, high-load capacity, ACK timing/persist retransmission cadence, gVisor receive autotuning, full Timestamp/PAWS and other wire variants remain outside this experiment.

### 14.28 True server-zero-window duplicate, overlap and post-reopen reordering oracle (2026-10-11)

**Fixed reference.** Continue to pin `ref/xray-core` at `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120` and `./xray` **26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64)**. Only the explicit, opt-in `XRAY_TUN_SERVER_RX_PRESSURE=1` branch of `scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` is expanded; ordinary namespace smoke and default `tun-gateway` paths remain unchanged. It still waits for a **real, persistently advertised server receive window 0**, rather than treating a brief outbound-staging zero-window ACK as persistent backpressure. The destination TCP/39648 remains paused until the raw sender releases it through the fixture's out-of-band file.

**New negative wire cases.** While the server advertises window zero, the sender now issues four single-byte segments carrying distinct high Timestamp values: first `SEQ=RCV.NXT, TSval=75000`, same-tuple duplicate at `SEQ=RCV.NXT, TSval=76000`, a future segment at `SEQ=RCV.NXT+8, TSval=77000`, and a one-byte overlap of previously acknowledged data at `SEQ=RCV.NXT-1, TSval=78000`. Each is checksum-valid; the fixture consumes response traffic and rejects RST or any cumulative ACK departure from the original expected sequence. After releasing the target's reads and requiring a real nonzero server-window update, the legitimate `SEQ=RCV.NXT, TSval=50000` retransmission must be ACKed with **TSecr=50000** (not any rejected high TSval).

**Gap recovery after reopening.** The same connection then receives an *out-of-order* four-byte tail first at `SEQ=next+8`, `TSval=85000`, with no advancement of the cumulative ACK permitted. Supplying its eight-byte prefix at the missing `SEQ=next` with `TSval=50010` must cumulatively ACK both, **TSecr=50010**; one more in-order byte at `TSval=50020` must yield **TSecr=50020**. The earlier in-memory `xray_tcp_actual_receive_zero_window_timestamp_probe` also now exercises the four rejected probes, post-reopen out-of-order repair, exact ACK/TSecr and full byte-for-byte delivery (including proof the rejected bytes never leak into the app stream); it remains ignored by default but is run explicitly for this slice.

**Observed paired Linux TUN result.** Both Chimera and pinned Xray 26.9.9 have already emitted matching success reports for these new tests: Chimera's stable zero occurred after **1,181,688** accepted bytes at server WS=3 and Xray's after **1,662,208** accepted bytes at WS=8 in this particular run. Buffer consumption is workload/scheduler-dependent, so the fixture checks the actual zero and reopening ACK instead of these numbers. Both rejected all four zero-window data segments without cumulative-ACK advance, recovered with **TSecr=50000**, absorbed the post-window missing-prefix gap with **TSecr=50010**, and accepted the next fresh byte with **TSecr=50020**. **No production TCP handler or reference code was modified**; only regression fixtures/documentation changed. This is a **single TCP flow** with deliberately omitted/reordered raw segments, not a stochastic packet-loss injector or concurrent-flow capacity test. Server-side zero-window retransmit timing beyond the bounded observation window, loss bursts, concurrent streams, autotuning and PAWS remain **Partial**. **Final verification:** `XRAY_TUN_SERVER_RX_PRESSURE=1 XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` **exited 0**, including the two new adversarial zero-window reports, eight Timestamp reports, eight Blackhole/TIME_WAIT reports, dual-stack routing/forwarding, XHTTP/H3, QUIC, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and strict TUN teardown. `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/watfaq-netstack/Cargo.toml` passed **25 unit + 64 standard integration** tests (two expensive cases ignored by default); the newly extended `xray_tcp_actual_receive_zero_window_timestamp_probe` and the independent 512-active-socket-capacity test were run explicitly and passed. `cargo test -p chimera_server_lib --lib --all-features tun_gateway --locked` passed **40** tests, strict `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings` passed, and both Rustfmt checks, Bash syntax and `git diff --check` passed.

### 14.29 Bounded receive-zero-window loss-burst and parallel-flow isolation oracle (2026-10-11)

**Goal and reference.** Retain pinned `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120` and locally built `./xray` 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64). The explicit opt-in `XRAY_TUN_SERVER_RX_PRESSURE=1 XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` continues to pause application reads on an independent destination TCP/39648 until the *actual server-side ACK window* stably advertises zero, keeping the fixture's transient-window recheck, bounded pressure, strict Timestamp validation and shutdown cleanup. Default nonstress namespace smoke, default `tun-gateway` and production TCP code remain untouched.

**Parallel isolation.** While one server-side Xray-TUN TCP connection is deliberately stalled at a real receive window of zero, the raw probe now opens **four independent kernel-TCP connections** (unique ephemeral tuples) in parallel to the normal echo target TCP/39643. Every connection must echo its own 128-byte payload in full within the bounded timeout, without cross-flow data contamination. It repeats with **four new connections** after the stalled connection's receive window has reopened and the first valid retransmitted byte has been cumulatively acknowledged. These additional flows are not independently forced into zero window; the claim is *isolation of unrelated traffic from one pressured stream*, not four simultaneously stalled streams.

**Controlled loss-burst/reordering after reopen.** After the valid retransmit with `TSval=50000`, five distinct contiguous four-byte chunks (`gap0`, `gap1`, `gap2`, `gap3`, `tail`) are represented by one shared twenty-byte TCP sequence span. The sender deliberately withholds chunk 0 and first transmits chunk indices **4, 2, 3, 1** using newer `TSval=85000+index` options, checking after each that no received ACK passes the missing four-byte prefix. This simulates a four-segment delivery/retransmission burst with specific reorder, but does **not** inject stochastic drops into a real network queue. It then sends the initial chunk using `TSval=50010`: the receiver must cumulatively ACK all 20 bytes with **TSecr=50010**; a new in-order one-byte `TSval=50020` segment must yield **TSecr=50020**. A previous high-TSval segment must not poison the recent TSecr, nor may the TCP stack deliver the gap too soon.

**Verification boundary.** The ignored-by-default `xray_tcp_actual_receive_zero_window_timestamp_probe` in the vendored netstack integration tests now exercises this four-chunk reordering after a *true server zero window*, verifies all exact ACK/TSecr values, and drains the complete application stream to prove correct reconstruction and absence of rejected zero-window probe bytes. Real Linux namespace validation applies the *same expectations* to Chimera and pinned Xray, but is kept an explicit opt-in expensive oracle. The compared test does not establish random packet-loss recovery, persist retransmit timing under sustained loss, concurrent *stalled* flows, long-duration load, receive autotuning or full PAWS compatibility. **Xray TUN remains Partial.**

**Final verified result.** `XRAY_TUN_SERVER_RX_PRESSURE=1 XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` exited **0** in the paired bounded oracle: Chimera reached stable zero after **1,179,640 bytes**, server WS=3, and Xray 26.9.9 after **1,659,136 bytes**, server WS=8 (these byte thresholds are not fixed assertions). Each implementation completed **4/4 independent TCP echoes while stalled and 4/4 after reopen**, preserved zero-window ACK under four rejected data variants, and completed the reordered four-chunk burst with **TSecr=50000 → 50010 → 50020**. The remainder of the full suite passed, including eight Timestamp reports, eight Blackhole/TIME_WAIT reports, dual-stack forwarding, QUIC/H3, ICMP/PMTU, route rollback and strict TUN shutdown. The vendored netstack passed **25 unit + 64 normal integration** tests; the expanded true-zero-window test passed separately and in **two additional repeated invocations**, as did the separately explicit **512-socket capacity** test. Chimera TUN library tests passed **40/40**, and strict workspace Clippy, both Rustfmt checks, Bash syntax and Git whitespace checks passed. No production Rust was modified in this follow-up. These results do not establish simultaneous stalled streams, randomly dropped traffic or TCP retransmission timing parity.

### 14.30 Paired simultaneous server receive-zero-window streams (2026-10-11)

**Scope and oracle.** Retain the exact reference `ref/xray-core` commit `52a412d9e2f5c2a5142b1b4e2ab3771dacb8b120` and local `./xray` 26.9.9 Custom (go1.27rc2 linux/amd64). The new *independently enabled* `XRAY_TUN_SERVER_RX_MULTISTALL=1 XRAY_BIN=./xray bash scripts/test_xray_tun_routes_netns.sh` mode takes the pre-existing optional server receive-pressure oracle on TCP/39648 and launches **two raw IPv6 TCP clients concurrently** against the same implementation. Each client has its own four-tuple (source TCP/46230 or /46231), initial SEQ, raw receive socket, nftables reset-suppression table and diagnostic log; neither can remove or shadow the other's packet-control state. A per-cohort private temporary directory contains two individually created `zero-<slot>` readiness markers. Each worker may signal readiness only **after its own server's actual, stable advertised receive window is 0**, the four invalid/duplicate/overlap data probes leave cumulative ACK unchanged and four independent normal TCP echo connections succeed. Only when **both** markers exist does the first worker release the shared paused downstream TCP readers; the second waits for this signal. Both clients then independently require a positive window update, successful accepted retransmission TSecr=50000, ordered repair of deliberately withheld/reordered four-byte data with TSecr=50010 and a fresh byte with TSecr=50020, as well as four additional independent echo flows. The two worker exit statuses, individual barrier and recovery reports are **both mandatory**, and any failure remains a hard nonzero result after completing the unrelated route/teardown checks.
